#10718. 7 Checks That Make Form Bugs Easier to Reproduce Across Teams

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer reports that a form’s error message overlaps its submit button. The developer opens the same page and sees no problem. Both are using the same code, but one has a narrow viewport, a translated interface, and an unusually long validation message.

A screenshot records what appeared on screen. A reproducible example also preserves the inputs, responses, permissions, and environment that produced it. That distinction matters when people review work at different times or join a project without knowing its undocumented conventions.

This article uses a workspace-invitation form as an example. The form accepts an email address and a role, submits a request, and displays either a confirmation or an error. The seven checks below turn its awkward states into something developers and designers can inspect together.

“The form looks broken” leaves too much for the next person to discover.

A useful report specifies the starting state, action, observed result, and expected behavior:

Include the browser, application revision, and relevant account permissions. Explain whether “360 pixels” means the browser’s layout viewport or the dimensions of an exported screenshot.

Avoid using real customer addresses in shared fixtures. A synthetic address such as member@example.test is sufficient to exercise the interface.

This report gives a designer a clear layout requirement and gives a developer a precise state to recreate.

A form tested only with successful submissions is missing most of its interesting behavior.

Create a small set of fixtures that cover the product’s actual rules:

Keep fixture values deterministic. Random addresses or constantly changing timestamps make visual comparisons harder to interpret.

Record expected outcomes alongside the data. A response containing a message does not establish where the interface should display it, whether focus should move, or which fields should remain editable.

Treat those as product decisions and test them explicitly.

Some failures can be reproduced by passing props to a component. Others depend on the request lifecycle.

Storybook args can describe inputs for isolated component states. An error-state story might supply a message, current field values, and a pending flag to a presentational form component.

However, passing pending: true does not prove that the real application enters or exits that state correctly.

For network-dependent behavior, use Mock Service Worker or your test runner’s request interception to supply a controlled response. Preserve the distinction between these two checks:

Avoid mocking the same endpoint in several overlapping layers. When a response is unexpected, the team should be able to identify which fixture produced it.

Also keep at least some integration coverage against the real API. A frontend mock that returns an outdated error shape can make the interface look reliable while hiding a broken contract.

Open the failing state at the recorded viewport. Inspect the error container, its parent layout, and the submit button.

Look for constraints such as fixed heights, absolute positioning, hidden overflow, and a flex or grid item that refuses to shrink.

A possible layout for a message beside an action is:

This pattern allows the message column to shrink and stacks the elements at a narrow width. It is not a universal fix: choose a breakpoint and arrangement that suit the surrounding form.

If the error belongs to a particular field, placing it beside the submit button may be the wrong design regardless of how well it wraps.

SitePoint’s browser-based CSS debugging guide explains how to inspect applied rules and layout behavior. Its guide to troubleshooting CSS with Inspect Element provides additional background.

Use DevTools experiments to identify the cause, then make the lasting change in the source. Check a wide viewport afterward so a narrow-screen fix does not introduce a desktop regression.

A screenshot can show that an error is present. It cannot, by itself, prove that the form preserved the email address or prevented an unintended navigation.

The following Playwright example assumes an existing application route, configured baseURL, and accessible form labels. Adapt the endpoint, response schema, and selectors to your project.

The example expects a native select for the role and a named form landmark. A custom role picker requires different interactions.

The behavior assertions precede the visual comparison. The screenshot then helps reviewers spot overlap, clipping, or unintended spacing changes.

Playwright’s visual comparison documentation explains baseline screenshots. Generate and compare them in a consistent environment; operating-system and font differences can affect rendering.

Review the first baseline manually. A screenshot test can faithfully preserve a bug if the initial image is approved without inspection. Disabling animation for a snapshot also does not test the accessibility of motion itself.

As a project grows, a component’s behavior should remain discoverable without a private walkthrough.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

The practical requirement for distributed contributors is a review process that carries its own context. Attach the exact fixture, preview location, reproduction command, and expected behavior to the issue or pull request.

Keep the open decision separate from the implemented behavior. This prevents reviewers from assuming that a visual change has already resolved a product question.

Name the person or team responsible for the component, and identify where design decisions are recorded. A stable fixture is useful only if someone maintains it as the component evolves.

A form that no longer overlaps can still be difficult to use.

Verify visible labels, keyboard navigation, focus visibility, and the relationship between errors and their fields. For field-level errors, consider aria-invalid and aria-describedby where appropriate.

The alert in the test example assumes a newly displayed form-level error. Do not add several competing live regions or announce every keystroke. Check the resulting experience with assistive technology.

SitePoint’s accessible form layout guide is relevant background for the relationship between structure and usability.

A focused toolset can support the workflow:

Use the smallest set that answers the current question. Adding another tool is useful when it closes a testing gap, not merely because it produces another report.

A good handoff lets someone unfamiliar with the original conversation recreate the failure, understand the expected behavior, and verify the fix. When the fixture and regression check remain with the code, that work continues to help the next contributor.

#10716. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10711. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10707. Quantum AI just got shockingly good at predicting chaos

A new study led by researchers at UCL (University College London) shows that combining quantum computing with artificial intelligence can significantly improve predictions of complex physical systems over long periods. The hybrid approach outperforms leading models that rely only on conventional computers.

The results, published in Science Advances, could enhance simulations of how liquids and gases behave, known as fluid dynamics. These types of models are essential in fields such as climate science, transportation, medicine, and energy production.

The improved accuracy appears to come from how quantum computers process information. Unlike traditional computers that use bits set to either 1 or 0, quantum computers use qubits, which can exist as 1, 0, or anything in between. In addition, each qubit can influence others, allowing a relatively small number of qubits to represent an enormous number of possible states.

Professor Peter Coveney, senior author from UCL Chemistry and the Advanced Research Computing Centre, explained the challenge: "To make predictions about complex systems, we can either run a full simulation, which might take weeks -- often too long to be useful -- or we can use an AI model which is quicker but more unreliable over longer time scales.

"Our quantum-informed AI model means we could provide more accurate predictions quickly. Making predictions about fluid flow and turbulence is a fundamental science challenge but it also has many applications. Our method can be used in climate forecasting, in modeling blood flow and the interaction of molecules, or to better design wind farms so they generate more energy."

Although quantum computers are widely expected to surpass classical machines in power, their real-world use has so far been limited. This new approach integrates quantum computing into a specific stage of the AI training process.

Typically, AI models learn from large datasets generated by simulations or observations. In this case, the data is first processed by a quantum computer, which identifies key statistical patterns that remain stable over time. These patterns, known as invariant statistical properties, are then used to guide the training of an AI model running on a conventional supercomputer.

The quantum-informed AI system delivered about 20 percent greater accuracy compared to standard AI models that did not use quantum-derived patterns. It also maintained stable predictions over longer periods, even when modeling chaotic systems.

Another major advantage was efficiency. The method required hundreds of times less memory, making it far more practical for large-scale simulations.

This performance boost comes from two defining features of quantum computing. Entanglement allows qubits to influence each other regardless of distance, while superposition enables a qubit to exist in multiple states at once until measured. Together, these properties allow quantum systems to process vast amounts of information in a compact form.

Demonstrating Practical Quantum Advantage

First author Maida Wang of the UCL Centre for Computational Science said: "Our new method appears to demonstrate 'quantum advantage' in a practical way -- that is, the quantum computer outperforms what is possible through classical computing alone. These findings could inspire the development of novel classical approaches that achieve even higher accuracy, though they would likely lack the remarkable data compression and parameter efficiency offered by our method. The next steps are to scale up the method using larger datasets and to apply it to real-world situations which typically involve even more complexity. In addition, a provable theoretical framework will be proposed."

Co-first author Xiao Xue, from Advanced Research Computing at UCL, added: "In this work, we demonstrate for the first time that quantum computing can be meaningfully integrated with classical machine learning methods to tackle complex dynamical systems, including fluid mechanics. It is exciting to see this kind of 'quantum-informed' approach moving towards practical use."

The researchers suggest that quantum computers are particularly well suited to modeling these systems because they can compactly represent their underlying physics. Many complex systems behave in ways that resemble quantum effects, where changes in one region can influence distant parts of the system, similar to entanglement.

Overcoming Limits of Current Quantum Hardware

Current quantum computers face challenges such as noise, errors, and interference, which often require large numbers of measurements. The new method avoids these issues by using the quantum computer only once during the workflow, rather than repeatedly exchanging data between quantum and classical systems.

The study used a 20-qubit IQM quantum computer connected to powerful classical computing resources at the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Germany.

To function, quantum computers must operate at extremely low temperatures, around minus 273C (close to absolute zero, colder than anything in space).

The research was funded by UCL and the UK's Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (EPSRC), with additional support from IQM Quantum Computers and the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Munich.

As researchers continue to scale up this approach, it could open the door to more accurate and efficient predictions across a wide range of scientific and engineering applications.

#10706. Building CRM Automations That Don't Break: APIs, Webhooks, and AI Validation

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Modern businesses rarely struggle with a shortage of software. The bigger problem is getting their existing systems to communicate properly.

A lead arrives through a website form, but the sales team doesn't receive a notification. Someone books an appointment, yet their CRM record isn't updated. A prospect replies to an automated message, but the follow-up sequence continues as though nothing happened.

These are integration problems rather than software problems.

Platforms such as GoHighLevel aim to solve them by combining contact management, messaging, appointment scheduling and workflow automation in one environment. However, the real value comes from how these components are configured and connected to other applications.

For developers, agencies and technical consultants, GoHighLevel provides an interesting example of how low-code automation and API-driven development can work together.

This guide explores the architecture behind an effective CRM automation system, how GoHighLevel's integrations operate, and where custom development makes sense. The patterns below — data normalisation, idempotent event handling, queue-based processing and validated AI outputs — apply to any CRM; GoHighLevel is used here as the running example because it combines native workflows with a public API and webhooks in one platform.

It's tempting to think of automation as a collection of simple rules.

When someone submits a form, send an email. When a customer books a meeting, update their status. When a payment arrives, trigger a notification.

Individually, these are straightforward tasks.

Problems emerge when different workflows start interacting.

Consider a business that generates leads through several channels:

Referral partners and external lead providers

Each channel introduces different information, sometimes in different formats.

One source might supply a full name, phone number and email address. Another provides only a phone number. A third might include campaign information, product preferences or qualification answers.

Without a consistent data structure, CRM records become fragmented. Duplicate contacts appear, important information disappears and automations begin acting on incomplete data.

A well-designed system needs to address these problems before adding more workflow complexity.

GoHighLevel combines native workflow tools with an API that allows developers to integrate external services.

Its core components include contact records, conversations, calendars, opportunities, pipelines and workflow automation.

Developers can interact with supported resources through the platform's REST API, while webhooks provide a mechanism for reacting to events.

Information enters the system through forms, funnels, integrations, inbound communication or API requests.

At this stage, the priority is collecting reliable information and associating it with the correct contact.

Once information has been received, workflows can evaluate conditions and initiate actions.

For example, a newly created lead might trigger an internal notification, a qualification sequence or an appointment invitation.

Some business processes require functionality beyond the CRM's native capabilities.

An organisation might need to synchronise an accounting platform, update an external database or pass information into a proprietary application.

This is where APIs, webhooks and middleware become especially useful.

The important architectural decision is determining which tasks belong inside the CRM and which should be handled externally.

GoHighLevel's developer platform offers REST endpoints for supported operations involving contacts, conversations, calendars, payments and other CRM resources.

Its API uses JSON and supports token-based authentication, including OAuth 2.0 for applications requiring delegated access.

For developers creating an internal integration, a Private Integration Token may be appropriate. Public applications serving multiple accounts generally require a different authentication architecture.

A basic API request follows a familiar pattern:

This example illustrates the structure of a contact-creation request. Production implementations should validate the current endpoint schema, manage tokens securely, handle retries and consider using an upsert operation when duplicate prevention is required.

Two production details deserve attention up front. First, treat a 429 rate-limit response as a normal control signal, not an error: back off exponentially, respect any retry-after value, and never retry in a tight loop. Second, prefer upserts keyed on a stable identifier (a normalised phone number or email address) over blind creates, so a retried request cannot spawn a duplicate contact.

The same principles apply when synchronising information from external applications.

For example, an insurance business might receive enquiries through a specialist quoting system. Rather than manually transferring those records, an integration could normalise the information and send it to the CRM.

The key is to treat the API as part of a broader data architecture, not merely as another way to trigger messages.

APIs are useful when an application needs to retrieve or modify information.

Webhooks address a different requirement: knowing when something has happened.

A webhook-driven architecture might look like this:

A configured webhook sends event data to an external endpoint.

The receiving application verifies and processes the event.

This is particularly useful when connecting CRM activity to internal dashboards, fulfilment systems or custom databases.

However, webhook integrations require careful handling.

A publicly accessible webhook endpoint shouldn't automatically trust every request it receives.

Where the platform provides signature verification, developers should validate the signature using the documented method before processing the payload. In practice this means computing an HMAC over the raw request body with the shared signing secret and comparing it to the signature header in constant time — never parsing the body first and re-serialising it, which can change the bytes the signature was computed over.

If an appointment event triggers an invoice, processing the same event twice could create duplicate invoices.

An idempotent handler should recognise an event that has already been processed and avoid repeating side effects. The reliable way to do this is to store each event's unique ID (or a hash of its payload) in a small processed-events table and check it before running any side effect — a duplicate delivery then becomes a cheap no-op instead of a second invoice.

Long-running operations shouldn't necessarily execute inside the initial webhook request.

A more resilient approach is to validate the event, record it, acknowledge receipt and place any substantial work on a processing queue. Return a 2xx response within a second or two so the sender does not retry, then let a worker handle the slow steps. Failed jobs should retry with exponential backoff and, after a set number of attempts, land in a dead-letter queue where a human can inspect them instead of being retried forever.

This helps prevent slow third-party services from disrupting event handling.

Consider a company receiving enquiries through paid advertising, website forms and telephone calls.

The objective is simple: respond quickly, qualify potential customers and book appointments.

A practical CRM workflow could follow several stages.

Incoming data should be converted into a consistent format.

Phone numbers should be standardised, source information should be preserved, and duplicate detection should be considered before creating another contact.

It's also important to retain communication preferences and the relevant consent information.

Not every lead should be distributed randomly.

Assignment logic might consider geographical territory, product category, team availability or an existing customer relationship.

For more complicated organisations, routing decisions may need to be calculated in an external service.

Once the lead has been assigned, the system can initiate an appropriate follow-up.

This might involve notifying a sales representative, sending a permitted acknowledgement or offering an appointment booking link.

The workflow needs to respond to changing circumstances.

If the prospect books an appointment, unnecessary booking reminders should stop.

If a sales representative takes ownership, automated outreach may need to pause.

If the prospect opts out, the system must respect that preference across relevant communication channels.

Finally, the system should record what happened.

Useful measurements include lead response time, appointment booking rate, appointment attendance and conversion rate.

These metrics make it possible to distinguish a workflow that simply runs from one that improves business operations.

Artificial intelligence introduces additional possibilities, particularly around lead qualification, conversation handling and information extraction.

An AI-powered system might analyse an incoming enquiry, identify the customer's requirements and suggest an appropriate next action.

It could also summarise a conversation for a sales representative or help classify enquiries into different service categories.

However, introducing an AI model creates new engineering considerations.

Allowing an AI model to generate unrestricted text is rarely sufficient when that output controls business processes.

A more predictable approach is to require structured responses and validate them before updating CRM records.

The application should check that the response matches the expected schema before performing any action.

Some actions are better handled by deterministic rules.

Communication consent, payment processing, account permissions and important customer record changes shouldn't depend solely on a language model's interpretation.

AI can help classify or summarise information, but critical operations should remain subject to validated rules and appropriate human oversight.

AI assistants can reduce repetitive work, but they should also recognise their limitations.

A prospect asking an unusual technical question or requesting a customised proposal may need to speak with a person.

The best automation systems provide clear escalation paths rather than trying to automate every possible interaction.

GoHighLevel's visual workflow builder makes many routine automations accessible to non-developers.

A basic appointment reminder or lead notification may require little custom development.

The situation changes when several systems need to work together.

For example, a business might need to migrate thousands of existing contacts, integrate custom booking software, synchronise records with an accounting platform and implement specialised lead routing.

These projects require more than familiarity with a visual workflow editor.

They involve data modelling, integration architecture, testing, security and operational reliability.

The decision rule is straightforward: use native workflows for anything the platform already does well, and write custom code only where the platform's model runs out — multi-source routing, external system synchronisation, or reporting that spans several platforms. That distinction matters because unnecessary custom development can make a system harder to maintain, while forcing every process into native workflows can create an equally fragile architecture.

Even technically capable teams can run into problems when implementing CRM integrations.

Large workflows containing dozens of branches are difficult to test and maintain.

Smaller, clearly defined workflows are generally easier to troubleshoot.

An integration that works with ten contacts may behave very differently when processing thousands of records.

Developers should account for rate limits, batch processing, exponential backoff and retry behaviour.

Creating a new contact for every incoming event can quickly damage CRM data quality.

Contact matching, normalisation and carefully selected identifiers help reduce duplication.

Access tokens should never be exposed in client-side JavaScript or committed to public repositories.

Use appropriate secret management and grant integrations only the permissions they require.

Successful execution doesn't necessarily mean the system behaves correctly.

Test scenarios should cover missing information, duplicate events, failed API requests, appointment cancellations, opt-outs and unexpected responses from external services.

Observability matters too. Logs and alerts should help identify which step failed and whether the operation can be retried safely.

Not every automation problem needs an API integration.

A useful decision framework is to start with the simplest reliable implementation.

Standard appointment reminders — Native CRM workflows

Basic lead assignment — Native workflows where supported

External application synchronisation — API or integration middleware

Complex routing across several data sources — Custom logic with CRM integration

AI-assisted lead classification — AI service with validated outputs

Enterprise reporting across multiple platforms — Dedicated reporting or data pipeline

Hybrid architectures are often the most practical.

The CRM manages customer interactions and standard business processes, while specialised services handle requirements that benefit from custom code.

This separation can also make future maintenance more manageable.

CRM automation has become considerably more accessible, but ease of implementation shouldn't be confused with reliability.

A workflow that sends a message after a form submission is easy to demonstrate. A system that handles thousands of contacts, prevents duplication, respects communication preferences, synchronises multiple applications and recovers gracefully from failures requires more careful design.

GoHighLevel provides a useful foundation for combining customer management with automation, particularly when its native capabilities are supported by well-designed integrations.

For developers, the opportunity isn't simply to automate more tasks. It's to design systems that remain understandable, maintainable and reliable as businesses grow.

The strongest implementations aren't necessarily the ones with the most workflows, API connections or AI features.

They're the ones where the right information reaches the right system at the right time — and everything continues working when something inevitably goes wrong.

#10703. Think AI "knows" what it’s doing? Scientists say think again

These are everyday words people use to describe what goes on in the human mind. But when those same terms are applied to artificial intelligence, they can unintentionally make machines seem more human than they really are.

"We use mental verbs all the time in our daily lives, so it makes sense that we might also use them when we talk about machines -- it helps us relate to them," said Jo Mackiewicz, professor of English at Iowa State. "But at the same time, when we apply mental verbs to machines, there's also a risk of blurring the line between what humans and AI can do."

Mackiewicz and Jeanine Aune, a teaching professor of English and director of the advanced communication program at Iowa State, are part of a research team that studied how writers describe AI using human-like language. This type of wording, known as anthropomorphism, assigns human traits to non-human systems. Their study, "Anthropomorphizing Artificial Intelligence: A Corpus Study of Mental Verbs Used with AI and ChatGPT," was published in Technical Communication Quarterly.

The research team also included Matthew J. Baker, associate professor of linguistics at Brigham Young University, and Jordan Smith, assistant professor of English at the University of Northern Colorado. Both previously studied at Iowa State University.

Why Human-Like Language About AI Can Be Misleading

According to the researchers, using mental verbs to describe AI can create a false impression. Words such as "think," "know," "understand," and "want" suggest that a system has thoughts, intentions, or awareness. In reality, AI does not possess beliefs or feelings. It produces responses by analyzing patterns in data, not by forming ideas or making conscious decisions.

Mackiewicz and Aune also pointed out that this kind of language can overstate what AI is capable of. Phrases like "AI decided" or "ChatGPT knows" can make systems seem more independent or intelligent than they actually are. This can lead to unrealistic expectations about how reliable or capable AI is.

There is also a broader concern. When AI is described as if it has intentions, it can distract from the humans behind it. Developers, engineers, and organizations are responsible for how these systems are built and used.

"Certain anthropomorphic phrases may even stick in readers' minds and can potentially shape public perception of AI in unhelpful ways," Aune said.

How News Writers Actually Use AI Language

To better understand how often this kind of language appears, the researchers analyzed the News on the Web (NOW) corpus. This massive dataset contains more than 20 billion words from English-language news articles published in 20 countries.

They focused on how frequently mental verbs such as "learns," "means," and "knows" were used alongside terms like AI and ChatGPT.

Mental Verbs Are Less Common Than Expected

The study found that news writers do not frequently pair AI-related terms with mental verbs.

While anthropomorphism is common in everyday speech, it appears far less often in news writing. "Anthropomorphism has been shown to be common in everyday speech, but we found there's far less usage in news writing," Mackiewicz said.

Among the examples identified, the word "needs" appeared most often with AI, showing up 661 times. For ChatGPT, "knows" was the most frequent pairing, but it appeared only 32 times.

The researchers noted that editorial standards may play a role. Associated Press guidelines, which discourage attributing human emotions or traits to AI, could be influencing how journalists write about these technologies.

Context Matters More Than the Words Themselves

Even when mental verbs were used, they were not always anthropomorphic.

For instance, the word "needs" often described basic requirements rather than human-like qualities. Phrases such as "AI needs large amounts of data" or "AI needs some human assistance" are similar to how people describe non-human systems like cars or recipes. In these cases, the language does not imply that AI has thoughts or desires.

In other cases, "needs" was used to express what should be done, such as "AI needs to be trained" or "AI needs to be implemented." Aune explained that these examples were often written in passive voice, which shifts responsibility back to human actors rather than the technology itself.

The study also showed that not all uses of mental verbs are equal. Some phrases move closer to suggesting human-like qualities.

For example, statements like "AI needs to understand the real world" can imply expectations tied to human reasoning, ethics, or awareness. These uses go beyond simple descriptions and begin to suggest deeper capabilities.

"These instances showed that anthropomorphizing isn't all-or-nothing and instead exists on a spectrum," Aune said

Overall, the researchers found that anthropomorphism in news coverage is both less frequent and more nuanced than many might assume.

"Overall, our analysis shows that anthropomorphization of AI in news writing is far less common -- and far more nuanced -- than we might think," Mackiewicz said. "Even the instances that did anthropomorphize AI varied widely in strength."

The findings highlight the importance of context. Simply counting words is not enough to understand how language shapes meaning.

"For writers, this nuance matters: the language we choose shapes how readers understand AI systems, their capabilities and the humans responsible for them," Mackiewicz said.

The research team also emphasized that these insights can help professionals think more carefully about how they describe AI in their work.

"Our findings can help technical and professional communication practitioners reflect on how they think about AI technologies as tools in their writing process and how they write about AI," the research team wrote in the published study.

As AI continues to develop, the way people talk about it will remain important. Mackiewicz and Aune said writers will need to stay mindful of how word choices influence perception.

Looking ahead, the team suggested that future studies could explore how different words shape understanding and whether even rare uses of anthropomorphic language have a strong impact on how people view AI.

Materials provided by Iowa State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10698. Artificial neurons successfully communicate with living brain cells

Engineers at Northwestern University have created printed artificial neurons that go beyond imitation and can directly interact with real brain cells. These flexible, low-cost devices produce electrical signals that closely resemble those generated by living neurons, allowing them to activate biological brain tissue.

In experiments using slices of mouse brain, the artificial neurons successfully triggered responses in real neurons. This result shows a new level of compatibility between electronic devices and living neural systems.

Toward Brain Interfaces and Energy-Efficient AI

This advance moves researchers closer to electronics that can directly interface with the nervous system. Potential uses include brain-machine interfaces and neuroprosthetics, such as implants that could help restore hearing, vision, or movement.

The technology also points toward a new generation of computing systems inspired by the brain. By replicating how neurons communicate, future hardware could perform complex tasks using far less energy. The brain remains the most energy-efficient computing system known, and scientists hope to apply its principles to modern technology.

The study will be published on April 15 in the journal Nature Nanotechnology.

"The world we live in today is dominated by artificial intelligence (AI)," said Northwestern's Mark C. Hersam, who led the study. "The way you make AI smarter is by training it on more and more data. This data-intensive training leads to a massive power-consumption problem. Therefore, we have to come up with more efficient hardware to handle big data and AI. Because the brain is five orders of magnitude more energy efficient than a digital computer, it makes sense to look to the brain for inspiration for next-generation computing."

Hersam is an expert in brain-inspired computing and holds multiple roles at Northwestern University, including the Walter P. Murphy Professor of Materials Science and Engineering at the McCormick School of Engineering. He also is a professor of medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine and a professor of chemistry at the Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences. In addition, he serves as chair of the department of materials science and engineering, director of the Materials Research Science and Engineering Center, and a member of the International Institute for Nanotechnology. He co-led the study with Vinod K. Sangwan, a research associate professor at McCormick.

Why the Brain Outperforms Traditional Silicon

Modern computers handle increasing workloads by packing billions of identical transistors onto rigid, two-dimensional silicon chips. Each component behaves the same way, and once manufactured, the system remains fixed.

The brain works very differently. It consists of many types of neurons, each with specialized roles, arranged in soft, three-dimensional networks. These networks are constantly changing, forming and adjusting connections as learning occurs.

"Silicon achieves complexity by having billions of identical devices," Hersam said. "Everything is the same, rigid and fixed once it's fabricated. The brain is the opposite. It's heterogeneous, dynamic and three-dimensional. To move in that direction, we need new materials and new ways to build electronics."

Although artificial neurons have been developed before, most produce overly simple signals. To achieve more complex behavior, engineers typically need large networks of devices, which increases energy use.

Printable Materials Enable Brain-Like Behavior

To better replicate real neural activity, Hersam's team built artificial neurons using soft, printable materials that more closely match the brain's structure. Their approach relies on electronic inks made from nanoscale flakes of molybdenum disulfide (MoS2), which acts as a semiconductor, and graphene, which serves as an electrical conductor. These materials were deposited onto flexible polymer surfaces using aerosol jet printing.

Previously, researchers treated the polymer in these inks as a flaw because it interfered with electrical performance. As a result, they removed it after printing. In this work, the team used that same feature to enhance the device.

"Instead of fully removing the polymer, we partially decompose it," he said. "Then, when we pass current through the device, we drive further decomposition of the polymer. This decomposition occurs in a spatially inhomogeneous manner, leading to formation of a conductive filament, such that all the current is constricted into a narrow region in space."

That narrow conductive path produces a sudden electrical response similar to a neuron firing. The resulting device can generate a wide variety of signals, including single spikes, continuous firing, and bursting patterns, closely resembling real neural communication.

Because each artificial neuron can produce more complex signals, fewer components are needed to perform advanced tasks. This could significantly improve computing efficiency.

Testing Artificial Neurons on Real Brain Tissue

To evaluate whether the artificial neurons could truly interact with living systems, the researchers partnered with Indira M. Raman, the Bill and Gayle Cook Professor of Neurobiology at Weinberg. Her team applied the artificial signals to slices of mouse cerebellum.

The results showed that the electrical spikes matched key biological properties, including their timing and duration. These signals reliably activated real neurons and triggered neural circuits in a way similar to natural brain activity.

"Other labs have tried to make artificial neurons with organic materials, and they spiked too slowly," Hersam said. "Or they used metal oxides, which are too fast. We are within a temporal range that was not previously demonstrated for artificial neurons. You can see the living neurons respond to our artificial neuron. So, we've demonstrated signals that are not only the right timescale but also the right spike shape to interact directly with living neurons."

Low-Cost, Sustainable Manufacturing and AI Implications

Beyond performance, the new approach offers environmental and practical advantages. The manufacturing process is simple and inexpensive, and the additive printing method places material only where it is needed, reducing waste.

Improving energy efficiency is especially important as artificial intelligence systems grow more demanding. Large data centers already consume vast amounts of power and require significant water for cooling.

"To meet the energy demands of AI, tech companies are building gigawatt data centers powered by dedicated nuclear power plants," Hersam said. "It is evident that this massive power consumption will limit further scaling of computing since it's hard to imagine a next-generation data center requiring 100 nuclear power plants. The other issue is that when you're dissipating gigawatts of power, there's a lot of heat. Because data centers are cooled with water, AI is putting severe stress on the water supply. However you look at it, we need to come up with more energy-efficient hardware for AI."

The study, "Multi-order complexity spiking neurons enabled by printed MoS2 memristive nanosheet networks," was supported by the National Science Foundation.

Materials provided by Northwestern University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10696. How to Create Effective Cold Email Sequences That Generate Replies: A Developer's Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting with prospects via cold email isn't always straightforward, particularly when recipients receive dozens of messages every day. Even a relevant email can get lost in a crowded inbox. That's why many B2B companies use cold email sequences to maintain consistent communication and manage follow-ups.

However, creating effective email sequences involves more than writing messages and choosing sending intervals. Developers building or integrating email automation systems must also consider API reliability, message scheduling, data validation, email authentication, security, and workflow monitoring.

A well-designed email sequence combines relevant communication with reliable technical infrastructure. This article explains how to structure cold email sequences and explores the development practices that make automated email workflows more dependable.

A cold email sequence is a planned series of emails sent to prospective customers who have not previously established a direct relationship with the sender. These messages are delivered over a defined period, with each email designed to introduce an offer, provide additional context, address a potential concern, or encourage a response.

For example, a software development company might contact a growing SaaS business to discuss opportunities to improve its application infrastructure. The first email could introduce the company's expertise, the second could explain a relevant technical challenge, and the third could share a useful observation about application performance.

Each message supports the same overall objective while contributing something different.

From a developer's perspective, a cold email sequence can also be understood as an event-driven workflow. Every contact has a sequence state, scheduled messages, and conditions that determine whether the next message should be delivered.

A basic sequence might follow this structure:

This model is useful for developers working on CRM integrations, email platforms, customer engagement systems, and automated business workflows.

The first step is to establish a clear plan before writing any messages. Determine who you want to contact, what problem your offer solves, and what action you want recipients to take.

Start by defining your ideal customer profile, identifying relevant decision-makers, and researching their business needs. Next, select one primary campaign objective, such as generating positive replies, booking qualified meetings, or exploring potential partnerships.

Decide how many emails you will send, what each message will communicate, and how much time will pass between follow-ups. Make sure every email adds value instead of repeating information from previous messages.

For example, a software consultancy targeting B2B SaaS companies might introduce an application performance challenge in the first email, share a practical recommendation in the second, provide a relevant engineering case study in the third, and close the sequence with a polite final follow-up.

Before launching the campaign, review the prospect list, verify contact information, check email authentication, and test the complete sequence.

Developers should also establish technical requirements before implementation:

Define how contact information will be stored and validated.

Determine which events trigger scheduled emails.

Implement conditions that prevent unnecessary follow-ups.

Establish secure API connections between applications.

Record delivery results, failures, and recipient responses.

These considerations help transform a basic email campaign into a maintainable automation workflow.

Every cold email sequence needs a specific objective. Without one, messages may contain several unrelated offers or ask recipients to take different actions.

A SaaS company might want to schedule product demonstrations, while a software consultancy may want to connect with businesses planning infrastructure improvements. A recruitment firm might aim to establish relationships with technical hiring managers.

Your objective should influence the message and call to action.

For developers building campaign dashboards, goals should also be represented through measurable application events.

This example demonstrates how a dashboard might calculate a positive reply rate from stored campaign events.

In production, developers should also handle zero denominators, duplicate events, inconsistent reporting periods, and differences between accepted, delivered, and failed messages.

Effective cold email sequences begin with the right audience. Even a well-written email is unlikely to generate meaningful replies if it reaches people who have no interest in the subject.

Start by defining your ideal customer profile. Consider industry, company size, location, business model, growth stage, and the challenges your product or service addresses.

For example, a development consultancy offering cloud migration assistance might contact CTOs, engineering managers, and technical founders. Although these professionals may benefit from similar services, their priorities can differ.

A CTO may focus on infrastructure reliability and operational costs, while an engineering manager may prioritize deployment workflows, maintainability, and team productivity.

Segment prospects according to meaningful differences rather than sending identical messages to everyone.

Audience segmentation can be implemented through database queries, CRM filters, or API-based contact management.

A simplified contact object might look like this:

Structured data makes segmentation more predictable and helps prevent inconsistent personalization.

Contact data should be collected and processed lawfully, with appropriate access controls and retention policies.

Personalization helps prospects understand why you are contacting them specifically. However, effective personalization involves more than inserting a first name, company name, or job title into a template.

Research relevant professional information that connects the recipient's business to your offer.

For example, a development consultancy contacting a SaaS company could mention a recently announced application launch and explain how scalable deployment practices may support future growth.

The observation should be accurate and connected to a genuine technical challenge.

A practical personalization process involves three steps:

Identify a relevant professional observation.

Explain why it may matter to the recipient.

Connect it naturally to the proposed solution.

Developers can support this process with reusable templates and structured variables.

This simple example demonstrates template personalization.

In production, developers should validate template variables, prevent accidental disclosure of customer data, and escape untrusted values when generating HTML.

The subject line gives recipients an initial indication of what an email contains. A clear, relevant subject line helps them recognize the message's purpose.

Keep subject lines concise and connected to the recipient's business needs. Avoid exaggerated promises, artificial urgency, and misleading claims.

Ideas for improving deployment reliability

A question about your engineering process

The best subject line depends on the audience and purpose.

Developers building email systems should also consider subject-line encoding, Unicode handling, template validation, and protection against header injection.

For example, email libraries should be responsible for constructing message headers rather than applications manually concatenating untrusted header strings.

SitePoint's tutorial on sending email using Node.js provides additional guidance on email construction and delivery.

The first email introduces your business and gives the prospect a reason to consider your offer.

A useful structure includes a personalized opening, a clear value proposition, a brief explanation of relevant expertise, and one simple call to action.

For example, a development consultancy contacting a SaaS business might explain that it helps software teams improve deployment workflows and application reliability.

Avoid introducing every service you offer or listing multiple benefits in the same email.

From a technical perspective, the first message should also establish the recipient's sequence state.

This state information allows a background worker to determine when an email is eligible for delivery.

Before sending, the worker must recheck the latest recipient status. A scheduled job should never override a newly recorded opt-out.

Subject: A question about your development workflow

I noticed [relevant observation about the company's software development process] and thought there might be an opportunity to improve deployment reliability.

Our team works with growing software companies on application infrastructure, development workflows, and performance improvements.

Is improving your development infrastructure a priority for your team this quarter?

The personalized observation should be specific and accurate. A concise message is more useful than a generic introduction filled with broad claims.

An effective cold email sequence follows a logical progression. Each message builds on the initial contact by introducing new information, clarifying value, or offering another way to engage.

Email sequencing tools can help teams schedule messages and manage follow-ups consistently. For developers integrating these systems into existing applications, it is important to understand how scheduling, contact states, reply events, and automation rules interact.

A five-email sequence can provide a practical starting point:

These intervals are starting points rather than universal rules. Adjust them to suit the audience, recipient preferences, and campaign performance.

For developers, scheduling is one of the most important technical aspects of email automation.

Sending messages directly inside an HTTP request can create unnecessary delays and make failure handling difficult.

A more reliable architecture uses background jobs.

This approach separates campaign management from email delivery.

For example, a worker can process scheduled messages independently while the main application continues handling user requests.

SitePoint's guide to implementing a task queue in Node.js explains how background processing can support workloads such as email delivery.

Production implementations should include persistent scheduling, retry policies, rate limiting, and idempotency controls to reduce duplicate messages.

Follow-up emails give prospects another opportunity to respond when they overlook an initial message or need additional information.

Each follow-up should introduce a useful detail, clarify the offer, or address a relevant concern.

For example, a software consultancy could use its first follow-up to explain a common deployment bottleneck. A later message might share a relevant case study or describe how the consultancy approaches application monitoring.

These practices can help development teams identify problems earlier and improve release reliability.

Stop the sequence when someone replies, opts out, or otherwise indicates that further contact is unwelcome.

Prospects receiving unsolicited emails may have little reason to trust an unfamiliar sender.

Relevant evidence helps establish credibility.

Social proof can include verified customer results, technical case studies, industry experience, or examples of previous work.

For example, a software consultancy might share a documented case study showing how automated testing reduced deployment failures.

When case studies are unavailable, demonstrate expertise through a practical observation or useful recommendation.

Developers building campaign systems should ensure that reusable case studies and message templates are versioned appropriately. This makes it easier to identify which content was sent and prevents outdated claims from being reused unintentionally.

Avoid exaggerated claims, invented statistics, and guarantees that cannot be supported.

A call to action tells the recipient what to do next.

In cold email outreach, a complicated request can discourage engagement because the prospect has not yet developed enough interest to commit time or resources.

Developers designing email templates should also consider accessibility. Links should use descriptive text, buttons should have sufficient contrast, and HTML emails should include readable plain-text alternatives.

Beyond content and scheduling, developers need to ensure that automated email workflows operate securely and consistently.

Email authentication helps receiving systems evaluate whether a message is authorized to use its sending domain.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework): Identifies servers authorized to send mail for a domain.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail): Uses cryptographic signatures to help verify message integrity and signing-domain responsibility.

DMARC (Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance): Defines how SPF or DKIM authentication should align with the visible From domain and provides reporting capabilities.

Developers responsible for email infrastructure should understand how these mechanisms work together.

SitePoint's article on email deliverability provides additional context about authentication, sender reputation, and delivery reliability.

Modern email workflows often integrate CRM systems, databases, automation platforms, and email service providers.

REST APIs allow developers to exchange information between these applications.

For example, an application might synchronize contact records, create campaigns, retrieve reply events, or update sequence states.

Avoid exposing sensitive information in logs.

These practices improve maintainability and reduce the risk of inconsistent campaign data.

A production webhook endpoint must verify the provider's authentication or signature mechanism before processing events. It should also handle duplicate deliveries, out-of-order events, and temporary database failures.

Testing is essential when an application sends automated messages.

Messages are scheduled at the correct times.

Failed jobs do not create duplicate messages.

Templates render correctly across email clients.

API failures produce useful operational logs.

Tools such as Postman, Mailpit, and automated integration tests can help validate different parts of the workflow.

Use synthetic recipients and controlled test environments rather than sending test campaigns to real prospects.

Developers building campaign dashboards or CRM-connected applications may need to integrate an external sequencing platform rather than implement every sending feature themselves.

EmailBison is one example of a platform for managing cold email campaigns and follow-ups. Its API and integration capabilities make it relevant when evaluating how a third-party email system can fit into an existing application architecture.

For developers, the main consideration is not simply whether a platform can schedule messages, but how its capabilities interact with the application's data model and operational requirements.

This architecture introduces several engineering considerations.

Data synchronization: Contact records must remain consistent between the CRM and the email platform.

Error handling: API failures should be recorded and retried safely when appropriate.

Security: API credentials and contact information should be protected.

Observability: Developers should be able to investigate failed operations without exposing sensitive information.

The same architectural principles apply to other email automation providers. Before choosing an integration, review its current API documentation, authentication model, event coverage, rate limits, and data-handling requirements.

Several common mistakes can reduce the effectiveness and reliability of cold email outreach, even when businesses use automation.

Sending generic messages: Emails that could apply to any business fail to establish relevance. Use appropriate segmentation and accurate personalization.

Repeating the same pitch: Every follow-up should contribute something new instead of repeating the original message.

Making emails too long: Excessive explanations and multiple offers make it harder for prospects to identify the main point.

Using aggressive follow-ups: Sending messages too frequently can frustrate recipients. Choose reasonable intervals and respect recipient preferences.

Making unsupported claims: Exaggerated results and misleading promises damage credibility.

Ignoring recipient responses: Stop automated sequences when someone replies, opts out, or requests no further contact.

Failing to handle duplicate events: A webhook may be delivered more than once. Applications should use idempotent processing to avoid repeated actions.

Ignoring API failures: Temporary provider outages and rate limits should not silently corrupt campaign state.

Insufficient testing: Automation workflows should be validated with controlled test data before production deployment.

Scaling too quickly: Increasing sending volume before validating deliverability, infrastructure, and workflow reliability can amplify existing problems.

Avoiding these mistakes helps teams maintain a professional communication process while reducing operational risks.

A cold email sequence can start with three to five emails, including the initial message and follow-ups. The appropriate number depends on the audience, purpose, and recipient preferences.

Focus on providing useful information rather than increasing the number of messages unnecessarily.

Human oversight remains important for message quality, data accuracy, security, and responding appropriately to interested prospects.

From a development perspective, enterprise integrations may also require stronger access controls, audit logging, and data synchronization.

Creating effective cold email sequences requires relevant messaging, thoughtful follow-ups, and reliable automation.

For developers, the underlying technical systems are just as important as the messages themselves. Contact validation, background scheduling, API integrations, email authentication, webhook processing, and error handling all contribute to dependable email workflows.

By treating email sequencing as a structured, event-driven application process rather than simply a series of scheduled messages, development teams can build systems that are easier to test, monitor, maintain, and extend.

The goal is not merely to send more emails. It is to create a reliable communication workflow that respects recipients and supports meaningful conversations.

#10694. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

#10690. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.
