#10649. Generators and Iterators in JavaScript: Lazy Data and Async Flows

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most JavaScript code loads data first and processes it afterward. That works for a list of ten items, but it falls apart when the list has a million entries, never ends, or arrives one network page at a time. Generators and iterators offer another approach: produce values only when someone asks for them. This article explains the iteration protocol, shows how generators build on it, and finishes with async generators for paginated data.

An iterator is any object with a next() method. Each call returns an object with two properties: value, the next item, and done, a boolean that becomes true when the sequence ends. An iterable is an object that hands out an iterator through a method keyed by Symbol.iterator. Arrays, strings, Maps and Sets are all iterables, which is why for...of, spread syntax and destructuring work on them.

Spread syntax calls [Symbol.iterator]() once, then calls next() until done is true. The code is correct but noisy. You manage the state yourself, and every new sequence needs the same scaffolding.

A generator function, declared with function*, returns an iterator without the manual bookkeeping. The yield keyword pauses the function and sends a value to the caller. The next call to next() resumes execution exactly where it stopped, with all local variables intact.

Calling countdown(3) does not run the body. It creates a generator object, and the body runs in slices as the consumer asks for values. Generator objects are iterable too, so spread and for...of work on them directly. The suspended state is the whole trick: the function body acts as a small state machine that the engine writes for you.

Values can also flow back in. The argument passed to next() becomes the result of the yield expression:

The first call to next() only runs the generator up to its first yield, so any argument passed to it is ignored. That detail trips up many developers.

Laziness is where generators earn their place. Array methods such as map and filter process the whole array at each step and allocate a new array each time. A generator pipeline handles one item at a time through the entire chain, which makes it possible to work with sequences that have no end:

The naturals generator never finishes, yet the pipeline completes because take stops asking after four results. No array of squares is ever built, and no number beyond 49 is computed.

There is a useful cleanup rule here. When a for...of loop exits early, through break, return or a thrown error, it calls the iterator's return() method. For a generator, that runs any finally blocks and ends the sequence. In the pipeline above, finishing take releases the generators upstream as well, so a generator that holds a file handle or a database cursor can close it inside a finally block.

Real applications spend more time waiting than computing, and async generators bring the same lazy model to asynchronous work. Declare one with async function* and consume it with for await...of. Each iteration waits for a promise to settle before the loop body runs.

A paginated API is a natural fit. Assume each response contains an items array and a nextUrl property that is null on the last page. This generator requests one page at a time and yields its items:

Once collectTitles has enough titles, the return statement ends the loop, which calls return() on the async generator. No further requests are made. Without a generator you would write a recursive function or a manual loop with its own stopping logic, and callers would have no say over the pace.

Async iteration also suits streams. In Node.js, readable streams are async iterable, so for await...of lets you process a large file chunk by chunk instead of holding all of it in memory.

Iterators define how to walk a sequence, generators make writing them painless, and async generators extend the same idea to data that arrives over time. Start small: replace one function that builds a large intermediate array with a generator, or one manual pagination loop with an async generator. The pull-based model keeps memory use predictable and gives the consumer control over how much work gets done.

#10648. NIKKE players told to scan their PCs after hackers used the game’s launcher to spread malware

In an official announcement, the publisher said an open-source domain used by some of its services was hijacked on September 27. Launcher script files were then partially modified, with the distribution of malicious programs beginning on October 30.

The issue was detected on November 4, after which Level Infinite replaced the affected files and refreshed its content delivery network. The publisher says the currently distributed version of the launcher is safe to use, but players who opened the Windows client after September 27 at 15:41 UTC+9 are being asked to perform a security check. Mobile-only players are not affected.

The updated PC client includes a security feature designed to detect and remove malicious files linked to the incident. Players are advised to close the game, launcher, and all browsers before starting the check.

Level Infinite recommends changing passwords for accounts used on the affected PC, as well as revoking API keys and access tokens. Those steps should be completed from a different device or after the PC has been secured.

#10646. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.

Materials provided by University of Barcelona. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10644. Nothing OS 5.0 (Android 17) is now rolling out to Nothing 4a and 3a series phones

Nothing Phone 3a, 3a Pro, 4a, and 4a Pro owners are in for a treat with the Nothing OS 5.0 update, now rolling out to all four phone models globally.

The Nothing OS 5.0 update began rolling out to Nothing Phone (3) owners just a few weeks ago, and now the stable build is ready for Nothing’s more budget-minded phones. The Nothing Phone (3a), Nothing Phone (3a) Pro, Nothing Phone (4a), and Nothing Phone (4a) Pro are now in line to get the big update, which includes a slew of new features alongside all the goodies that come with Android 17.

As is the case with all firmware updates, the Nothing OS 5.0 update is rolling out in batches to owners of the aforementioned four Nothing Phone models, so if you don’t see the update right away, just keep an eye out for the notification over the next few days.

Alternatively, you can navigate to system settings, scroll down to system, tap system updates, and then hit the check for updates button. My Nothing Phone (4a) Pro doesn’t have the Nothing OS 5.0 update yet, so be aware that yours might not either.

The Nothing OS 5.0 update includes a new Glyph app, which offloads the Glyph Interface features to a dedicated app with a tweaked UI. #10640. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

Five Ways Quantum Mechanics Affects Everyday Life

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Original written by Lesley Henton. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10635. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."

Materials provided by University of Illinois Grainger College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10632. How to Source Passive Candidates with AI in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Passive candidates answer when a message is specific, short and clearly about them. This is a nine-step playbook, and each step shows how to do it in Pin. The method works in any tool. For a shorter version, see Pin's 7-step guide to sourcing passive candidates.

For recruiters sourcing passive talent at scale, Pin covers the workflow end to end. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and ranks them against your brief, and the recruiter makes every call.

A passive candidate is a working professional who isn't applying for jobs but might say yes to the right conversation.

Gallup's fourth-quarter 2025 panel sizes the pool. It found that 51% of U.S. workers are actively looking (11%) or watching for opportunities (40%).

The practical reading: only 11% of workers are actively looking, so most of the market isn't on a job board, and about half is paying attention.

Write the brief as outcomes and skills, not a title. Use five lines:

Skills-based pools are larger. LinkedIn's Economic Graph estimated in March 2025 that defining eligibility by skills rather than prior job title would expand the typical talent pool by a median 6.1x globally and 15.9x in the United States. That is a modeled estimate, with "eligible" meaning at least half of the target job's top skills.

In Pin: paste the job description, add a job listing link, upload two or three ideal résumés, or import the role from your ATS. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Answer them. They are the parts of the brief you hadn't written down.

Pin says its AI sourcing searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, aggregated from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Ryan Levy, Managing Partner at Cruit Group, says: "Pin gave us the ability to find candidates that didn't appear on LinkedIn Recruiter. The platform is easy to use and is continuing to evolve!"

Here is the same search written two ways. Pin's Boolean search guide for recruiters covers the operators in the first version.

("senior data engineer" OR "staff data engineer" OR "lead data engineer") AND (Snowflake OR BigQuery OR Redshift) AND (dbt OR Airflow) AND (fintech OR payments OR "financial services") NOT (intern OR junior OR consultant OR recruiter)

Senior data engineer who has built a warehouse from scratch at a Series B to D fintech. Strong dbt and Airflow. Remote in US time zones. Avoid consultancies.

The string misses anyone titled "Software Engineer, Data Platform" who has done the same work. Skills-based matching reads the work, so title mismatches stop hiding good people. Filters can be written in natural language, and advanced filters stay under your control.

Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, puts it this way: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

A ranked list is only useful if you can see why each person ranks. Pin gives every candidate a fit score and an AI-written reason. Read the reason first, then accept or reject.

Pin learns from accept and reject feedback across the team, so the second batch should look better than the first. If the reasons show the wrong kind of match, fix the brief rather than rejecting one person at a time.

Spend contact credits after the shortlist, not before it. Pin returns personal email, work email and phone number, at 1 credit each. The Free plan includes 5 credits a week. Solo includes 500 a month, and Professional includes 1,000 per seat a month.

Reference the person's actual work, give one reason this role might fit, and make one small ask. Keep it short. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's own profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice, so you edit rather than start blank. Email steps send automatically. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Pin's guide to engaging passive candidates goes deeper on message length and personalization.

The messages below are illustrative. They are templates with placeholders, not real results.

Hi [First name], I read your write-up on moving [Company]'s billing pipeline to dbt. The point about [specific lesson] is something we're working through too.

I'm hiring a senior data engineer at [Company]. The job is to [one-sentence outcome]. It's remote in US time zones.

Worth a 15-minute call to see if it's relevant? If not, no problem at all.

Message 2: follow-up in the same thread (about day 3)

Hi [First name], one detail I left out: [team size, comp range or what the first 90 days look like]. If the timing is wrong, tell me and I'll check back in a few months.

Message 3: an SMS task you send (about day 5, only where you have a number and the rules allow)

Hi [First name], [Your name] from [Company]. I emailed about a senior data engineer role. Worth a quick chat? Happy to stop here if not.

Pin reports that its multi-channel outreach sequences deliver 5x better response rates than industry averages, measured against LinkedIn's published 10-25% InMail reply benchmark. That figure is vendor-reported, so test it against your own baseline.

Follow-ups do most of the work. In Pin's Recruiting Outreach Benchmarks 2026, covering 4,000,000+ messages sent between June 2025 and May 2026, follow-ups earned 56.2% of all replies. The first three touches captured 93.2% of the replies a sequence earns, and a fourth raised that to 97.7%. Half of replies arrived within 4 hours and 74.8% within a day, so spacing touches about two business days apart gives each message its chance. The data comes from Pin's own platform, so treat it as a starting point.

A workable plan: three to four touches about two business days apart. Change the content each time, and add a channel when you have a legitimate way to reach the person. In the same data, sequences that paired email with a LinkedIn message were associated with 2 to 4x the replies of email alone at the same touch count. Recruiters add the LinkedIn touch for the candidates they want most, so part of that gap reflects prioritization.

Pin's multi-channel sequences combine automated email steps with AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Email sends on schedule. Pin queues each LinkedIn and SMS step as a task with the message drafted, and you send it. Sequences adapt per candidate, any reply pauses the sequence, and every channel for a candidate stays in one thread. A shared candidate pool with a dedupe layer keeps two recruiters from contacting the same person. The Free plan has email-only sequences. Multi-channel sequences start on Solo.

Passive candidates book around a day job, so offer early, late and lunchtime slots. Pin's AI interview scheduling handles time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, and confirmations land in the ATS.

Track positive replies separately, since a reply is not the same as interest.

Change one variable at a time, such as the opening line or the day of the follow-up, and compare within the same role. Pin's funnel dashboards show pipeline velocity, conversion by stage and activity by recruiter. Pin's 2026 user survey across 2,000+ organizations and 20,000+ users reports a 14-day average time-to-fill and 12 hours saved per recruiter per week. Test those vendor-reported figures against your own.

Once steps 1 to 8 work by hand, hand over the repetitive ones. Pin's AI recruiting agent has two modes. In Assist, you approve each step. In Autopilot, the agent sources, screens and ranks, drafts outreach, sends the email steps, follows up on silence, schedules interviews and updates the ATS. LinkedIn and SMS steps stay tasks that you send. Closing and offers stay human. For how other agents compare, see best AI recruiting agents.

Start in Assist on your first two roles and read every draft. Move email follow-ups and scheduling to Autopilot first, since they repeat most. Guardrails keep a human in the loop, every action appears in a feed, and the agent runs 24/7.

Pin also has an MCP server. It lets AI assistants such as Claude Desktop or ChatGPT set up searches, review candidates and tune ranking, and its page says outreach can't be sent silently.

Miles Randle, Head of People & Talent at Flip CX, says: "As a small people and talent team, we don't have a ton of time to spend hours sourcing and messaging. Pin has made it possible for us to focus on the people side of things!"

These channels are slow and hard to scale. Use them for senior and niche roles, and add the names to Pin so the dedupe layer prevents double outreach.

Start with a brief written around skills and outcomes, then search several sources rather than one network. #10631. Pixel 6 postmortem: How Google’s flawed reboot defined future hardware [Video]

I think there are few smartphones in the past decade that have arrived carrying the sheer weight of expectation that greeted the Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro in late 2021. Now on life support, just what did we learn from 2021’s reboot for the brand?

There was a lot of bubbling hype around ahead of Made by Google before the Pixel 6 was unveiled. I think a lot of that is due to just how flip-flop the Pixel lineup had become after years of identity crises, weird experimental gestures, compromised battery sizes, and following on from the charming but overtly mid-range Pixel 5, Google said enough was enough and finally decided to swing for the fences.

I think fans hoped that Google was done playing nice and coming second to Samsung and Apple. The Pixel 6 series was built to make a statement, introducing a brand new design language, custom silicon in the shape of Google Tensor, huge camera sensor upgrades, and a shockingly competitive price tag.

Yet, looking back with years of hindsight, the Pixel 6 duo remains one of the most polarizing Google releases, probably ever, but we can’t get away from the important fact that it laid the technological foundation for everything Pixel represents today, but it did so while putting early adopters through a gauntlet of software bugs, sluggish biometrics, thermal throttling, and atrocious cellular connectivity.

So with that in mind, this is the Google Pixel 6 and 6 Pro: the good, the bad, the ugly, and how this turbulent reboot shaped the future of Pixel.

Before the Pixel 6, Google’s hardware design wasn’t exactly tearing up trees. I’d say it was best described as understated, if not entirely apologetic. The fabric cases notwithstanding, and maybe the soft-touch bio-resin finish of the Pixel 5 aside, nothing about them screamed “I am a flagship phone” across a crowded room.

I genuinely feel that the Pixel 6 changed that overnight, but without losing some of the “playful” feel that has been a hallmark of basically all Google hardware.

Dominated by a bold, edge-to-edge camera visor across the rear, the design was, dare I say it, instantly iconic. Instead of an awkward off-center stove-top camera island that rocked incessantly when placed on a flat desk, the camera bar acted as a natural kickstand. It gave the Pixel line an unmistakable silhouette that Google continues to iterate on today.

One thing that was interesting at the time and even more so today is how the line was separated at launch. Two distinct yet similar designs with dual-tone pastel glass backs. The Pixel 6 had a bigger dash of “fun” with the Sorta Seafoam, Kinda Coral, and the striking Stormy Black. The 6 Pro leaned into more traditional executive styling with polished aluminum rails and curved front glass, whereas the standard Pixel 6 has that flat display glass, matte black borders, and a boxier aesthetic.

However, the hardware was not without ergonomic pain points. Both devices were heavy, slippery, and massive. Google completely abandoned the compact form factor of the Pixel 5, offering no refuge for small-phone purists after the palm-friendly 4a and even the smaller Pixel 4. The polished rails of the Pro were a magnet for grease, while the glossy back panels made both phones still feel like a wet soap bar without a protective case.

Complaints aside, the design was arguably the biggest standout. I think they still look great today.

The biggest architectural gamble with the Pixel 6 generation was the departure from Qualcomm Snapdragon processors in favor of in-house silicon. Before launch, we’d heard so much about the first-generation Google Tensor chip thanks to numerous leaks, but really had no idea what it would bring to the table.

Developed in partnership with Samsung’s Foundry, Tensor, or “Tensor G1” as it is known today, was not designed to beat Apple’s A-series chips or Qualcomm’s Snapdragon lineup on synthetic Geekbench benchmarks. Google’s main pitch was on-device machine learning.

The first-gen Tensor chip unlocked voice typing through Google Assistant that was fast and accurate enough to genuinely replace keyboard tapping, real-time live translation, Magic Eraser photo cleanup, and instant offline speech transcription. These are now commonplace, but they felt like real differentiators at the time.

In everyday UI tasks, the Pixel 6 felt fluid and remarkably responsive. Paired with Android 12 and the debut of the Material You’s dynamic theming engine, using the phone felt fresh, cohesive, and modern but styled with Google’s trademark flourishes.

The trade-off was efficiency. Fabricated on Samsung’s 5nm LPE node, the first Tensor generation struggled, well, struggled is an understatement, with sustained thermal performance. Under heavy loads or prolonged 4K recording sessions, the phone warmed up rapidly. Gaming performance was volatile, and battery life was wildly inconsistent between individual units.

I think it’s fair to say that those issues were the tip of the iceberg. The first six months of the Pixel 6’s lifecycle were marred by two notorious hardware bottlenecks: the optical fingerprint reader and the cellular modem.

Google made the controversial decision to ditch the ultra-reliable rear-mounted Pixel Imprint capacitive scanner in favor of an under-display optical sensor. You fell into a couple of camps with this problem in particular. I can’t say I had a single issue, save for unlocking being slower than the competition at the time, but for thousands of people, it was one of the biggest frustrations from the get-go.

Google issued multiple software patches attempting to tune recognition algorithms, but software can only do so much to compensate for mediocre optical hardware.

In my mind, the most damaging issue was cellular connectivity.

So instead of using Qualcomm’s class-leading modems, the Pixel 6 series tried out Samsung’s Exynos 5123 modem. You’ll know all about this if you use either phone for any extended period of time. Dropped calls, slow to send text messages, weak signal basically everywhere, even when in the visible range of a cell tower. It was a mess.

Even testing showed it was just bad compared to other flagship phones. It was a disaster, and this isn’t something you can ever fix with a software update.

Despite Android 12 being the biggest shift in the platform up until that point, and debuting on the Pixel 6 and 6 Pro, it was a very buggy update and one that needed near constant attention alongside the other issues this duo faced post-launch.

The situation spiraled after the disastrous December 2021 security update. The update introduced critical call-dropping bugs so severe that Google was forced to pull the release entirely and pause OTA rollouts for nearly a month while working on emergency fixes that pushed into late January 2022.

For a product line whose defining identity had always been pristine software and rapid, flawless monthly updates, the December-January release cycle was not a good look. That’s not discounting some early issues with the new and improved camera systems onboard.

For four straight generations prior, Google clung stubbornly to the aging 12.2-megapixel Sony IMX363 sensor. I’d say that the company was overrelying on computational photography wizardry to squeeze blood from a stone. With the Pixel 6 and 6 Pro, Google finally listened to the complaints and paired its legendary image pipeline with some actual cutting-edge optical hardware.

Both phones pack a 1/1.31-inch 50MP Samsung ISOCELL GN1 main sensor, which captures roughly 150% more light than the Pixel 5. The resulting images delivered that lovely natural optical shallow depth of field, stunning dynamic range, and rich textures that no longer relied on over-sharpening algorithms. Night Sight became noticeably faster, and skin tones were rendered with unprecedented fidelity thanks to the introduction of Real Tone tuning.

On the Pixel 6 Pro, Google added a dedicated 48MP 4x periscope telephoto lens. This transformed the Pro into a versatile long-range shooter, producing clean hybrid zoom up to 20x.

I guess like basically everything else on the Pixel 6 and 6 Pro, the transition to a larger physical sensor was not entirely smooth. Close-up shots frequently suffered from optical fringing and falloff around the edges of text documents, highlighting the lack of dedicated macro capabilities on the standard ultra-wide camera. Before updates, there was a ton of oversharpening that felt like a hangover from the previously 12MP main sensor era that had to be ironed out.

Video recording, while improved through HDRnet processing, still lagged behind the iPhone in low-light stability and frame consistency. Still, the Pixel 6 camera setup finally looked and shot like real flagship hardware.

In retrospect, the Pixel 6 was the messy blueprint that made everything that came after it possible. Google treated this generation as a clean break, and the hard lessons learned directly dictated the roadmap for subsequent flagships.

The industrial design was one of the strongest areas, and we’ve seen it refined into an integrated visor on the Pixel 7 and 8 series, eliminating the delicate glass visor seams and smoothing out corner transitions. The optical fingerprint reader, while still not top-tier ultrasonic until the Pixel 9 series, saw improved hardware revisions and accompanying software facial recognition fallback added to the next device.

On the connectivity front, the widespread backlash pushed Google to iteratively address modem efficiency and reception, moving through newer Samsung modems before laying the groundwork for better connectivity in recent years. The camera sensor’s higher baseline established on the 6 series provided the stable foundation for Google’s imaging algorithms to mature over several release cycles. The consistency is back and remains a strong suit of the latest phones once again.

My Seafoam Green P6 is still one of my favorite phones of all time. Having since gone to XL Pixels in the 3 phones after, I really love the more compact size and the in-hand feel of that phone that is so refreshing after using mini tablet size phones for so long.

The story of the P6 generation I think is one that Google has since learned from. It is the generation that basically took one for the Pixel project, the kind of reborn Pixel project that is being the first of the Tensor era phones.

Hardware wise you can see how each generation since has gradually gotten better in meaningful ways and while Google has been slow to respond to feedback and changes, Pixels albeit probably priced too high still, the P6 phones laid the groundwork for this new era and it was a solid effort I think many will agree even with all the documented problems.

Crucially, the aggressive $599 pricing of the base Pixel 6 in some ways reset consumer expectations across the industry. I don’t doubt that it proved premium hardware and custom silicon did not require a four-figure entry fee. A lot of that has been ditched in recent years, but the point remains.

The Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro were fundamentally flawed pioneers for Google. They were the firm’s most ambitious hardware undertaking to date, and simultaneously one of the most frustrating phone series to live with during their initial launch window and beyond.

If the Pixel 5 was a safe play ahead of this big pitch, the Pixel 6 was a risky dive into deep waters. It stumbled badly yet without those painful growing pains; the more polished, confident, and reliable Pixel devices we enjoy today simply would not exist.

#10630. Alan Turing's biggest AI assumption may have been wrong

Alan Turing's famous ideas about artificial intelligence may have sent AI research down the wrong path for the past 75 years, according to prominent computer scientist Peter J. Denning.

In his new book, Turing's Mistake: Escaping the Yoke of Unintelligent Machines, Denning argues that two foundational assumptions made by Turing in 1950 continue to shape AI research today. The first is that intelligence can exist independently of a physical body and therefore be recreated in computer software. The second is that a machine can demonstrate intelligence by successfully imitating a human in conversation, an idea that later became known as the Turing test.

"These two claims have shaped much of AI research and development," Denning writes. "My premise is that our acquiescence to these claims has led to the AI mess in which we find ourselves today."

Denning argues that pursuing artificial general intelligence (AGI), or machines with human level intelligence, is unlikely to succeed. Instead, he warns, the technologies society is building could introduce significant new risks.

At the heart of Denning's argument is the idea of tacit knowledge, the vast amount of human understanding that cannot easily be put into words or represented in a form that computers can process.

He says machine learning cannot capture five major categories of tacit knowledge: common sense, everyday interactions with people and the environment, emotions and perception, practical performance skills, and the social and historical knowledge embedded in culture.

Researchers have long attempted to organize common sense into databases. One of the best known efforts was Douglas Lenat's Cyc project, which began in the 1980s with the goal of creating an extensive collection of common sense facts. After four decades of work, the project contained roughly 25 million entries.

"Yet even this treasury could not add up to a background of common sense sufficient to make expert systems smart enough to be experts," Denning notes. "Cyc validated that much of the knowledge that makes people experts cannot be articulated as propositions."

Denning believes practical skills present an even greater challenge.

"Our performance skills in thousands of domains cannot be communicated to machines," he explains. "Whereas descriptions of skillful outcomes ('know what') can often be represented as bits and stored in a machine, we do not know how to encode the embodied knowledge for skillful performance ('know how')."

He points to accomplished musicians as an example.

"A virtuoso violinist can play beautiful music yet cannot describe to an acolyte how to produce it.

"Even if a robot could observe and imitate skilled humans, having no biological body, a robot cannot grasp how the musician feels when playing beautiful music or how an audience feels when hearing it."

Denning also includes intuition, gut feelings, imagination, and spontaneous creativity among the forms of tacit knowledge that remain beyond the reach of machines.

Denning argues that all of these limitations stem from what he calls the "representation problem."

Computers can only perform calculations using data and instructions that have been encoded into physical forms they can recognize and process. Tacit knowledge, however, does not naturally fit into that framework.

"Behind every word is a deep well of tacit knowledge that gives it meaning," Denning says. "Words are but symbolic representations of meanings, not the meanings themselves. Commonly used Large Language Models, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini only manipulate words, they cannot know or understand the meaning of what they are saying."

According to Denning, this creates a fundamental divide. Because scientists still cannot fully explain how tacit knowledge works in humans, they also cannot translate it into a form machines can use.

"How we host tacit knowledge is largely a mystery," Denning admits. "All we know is that it is embodied. We have no idea what we might observe and measure in our bodies to reveal it."

Denning also argues that intelligence depends heavily on context, the surrounding circumstances that give words, actions, and decisions their meaning.

Context allows people to recognize sarcasm, humor, sincerity, and emotion. It helps determine when to be diplomatic, when to joke, and how to interpret countless social cues.

"When you inquire into where an assumption of the current context came from, you discover it rests on previous conversations from previous contexts. Each of those in turn rests on further previous conversations and their contexts. This pattern is endless and fractal," Denning explains.

Culture presents another major obstacle for AI.

Denning describes culture as encompassing values, norms, judgments, history, communities, moods, and even relationships involving power and care.

"Human conversations are imbued with background assumptions that give meaning and relevance to the words being used," Denning explains.

"Scaling up LLMs with ever larger neural networks will not enable them to acquire the embodied human knowledge we call culture. LLMs will not attain the objective of the Turing test: to demonstrate machine thought indistinguishable from human thought."

AI Safety and the Limits of Human Understanding

Denning concludes that humans and AI systems may ultimately develop different forms of tacit knowledge that neither can fully understand.

"Machines cannot read our tacit knowledge and we cannot read theirs," he writes. "We are aliens across an uncrossable divide."

He argues this gap raises serious concerns about AI safety. If machines cannot interpret the unspoken context behind human intentions, reliably aligning advanced AI systems with human goals may prove impossible.

"Through AI automation, agentic networks of machines are likely to develop their own machine intelligence that does not reach the level of human general intelligence but is still quite capable of creating severe problems for humans. This threat is a greater than a take-over by superintelligent machines," he explains.

"Machine intelligence has different concerns from us and does not appear to care about us. Its ways of thinking and problem-solving look alien to us. We do not yet know how to live safely with these machines.

"Pulling back from an AI automation singularity will demand much from us. We start by accepting that the familiar culture is fading away as intelligent machines appear in our society and we do not know what is coming. We decline to think like machines or be subservient to machines. We refuse to submit to a yoke imposed by low-intelligence machines. Most importantly, we reassert our humanity, declare once again what makes us different from machines, and celebrate those differences."

Materials provided by Taylor & Francis Group. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10628. Dawn of War 4 may be the most generous RTS I've played

Maybe the best way to understand the kind of game Dawn of War 4 wants to be is through a single button. If you're playing a campaign mission in DoW 4 and you're not really feeling it, or you made a silly mistake right at the end and got summarily wiped by the AI, or maybe you're in a hurry and just really curious about what happens next in the story, you have a quietly revolutionary option to hand: you can just hit the 'skip mission' button, and move on.

Dawn of War 4's game director, Jan Theysen explained the thinking to me like this: "In the end, we feel it's not that we want the player to play, necessarily, the game we envision for them. It's more like: here is the thing you can play with, and have fun."

That line of thought seems to permeate Dawn of War 4 in all kinds of places, and in turn makes it come across as incredibly generous. My most recent time with the game, for instance, focused on the campaign and the skirmish mode. The campaign alone is massive. It has four sub campaigns within it, one for each faction, their stories linking to form one grand narrative surrounding the planet of Kronus - returning here from the original Dawn of War's cult favourite expansion, Dark Crusade. Any one of those four campaigns is, according to developer King Art Games, longer than the entire original Dawn of War campaign. And within each there's at least one fork in the road, where you make a choice leading you down one of two mission paths. In other words, each campaign is really almost two campaigns in one.

The two I've played - right the way to completion, making that about half the total game's campaign already - are those of the time-honoured kunnin' Orks, and the shiny new techno-Catholics of the Adeptus Mechanicus (Admech, to the 40k nerds). To pick one example of how these can split, the Orks' campaign initially focuses on Guzkutta - a local Kronus upstart with suitably big ambitions - and you're given the choice of sticking with him or switching to the glorious, returning warboss Gorgutz, who may or may not need knocking off his perch. The campaign that follows mixes wonderfully chaotic infighting between the two Ork factions, with a fair bit of Admech-stomping and human-squashing on the side.

The Admech campaign is similar. Without spoiling much, you begin playing as a deeply inquisitive Potentia-Delta-9, who goes digging around in Kronus' deserts without a sufficient remit. All that's laced with an array of delightful 40k-isms: Mysterious voices! Betrayal! Computer spirits! Outraged officials citing revered and highly verbose bureaucratic procedure! If you've read any of the 40k novels (or indeed the Horus Heresy; those mysterious voices will jump out to anyone familiar with the first couple of books) then you will be well at home here. Dawn of War 4 has nabbed John French, one of the Black Library's Horus Heresy authors, so perhaps none of this should come as a surprise.

These splits then play out smartly in the structure of missions themselves. Some might be a race, for instance, where you and your rival need to fight your way through to a final point beyond a single, powerful enemy's base. I found one version this format especially enjoyable in the Admech campaign, when those two separate, essentially linear paths converged at one point, creating a supremely 40k type of bottleneck as three warring factions all met at once. I found success by letting my rival battle the stronger Ork faction, then darting in to pick off their main army for a major setback, then brute force my way through the remaining, slightly softened Ork horde. Then I sould send my my newer reinforcements from my base back into that chokepoint for more stalling while what was left of my vanguard made a big push to the finish.

Back to the sense of generosity, meanwhile, and the other part of this comes in the skirmishes, where there's an extraordinary amount of customisability available. You can tinker with the game's underlying numbers to make units squishier or tankier by default, or to make research or point-capturing faster or slower. You can change income rates, reinforcement costs, cooldown times. By my count, there are nine 'general' settings, eight 'economy' ones, and eight 'combat' ones to play around with. You can choose from multiple commander units (or disable them or tinker with how they spawn), as well as multiple game modes. There's the classic Annihilation and Domination (wipe out the enemy or take and hold capture points) alongside new modes called Maelstrom and Defence. These have you racing to claim one-off capturable points that drop in periodically all around the map, and, I'm going to assume… defending something (only the first two modes were playable in this preview). Then there's the Last Stand mode, which returns here revamped from Dawn of War 2 - again unavailable in this build - and the army painter; a classic feature which I can't talk about for now. Plus, reams of post-launch stuff to do, from free maps to new modes such as my personal beloved: the Conquest mode popularised in Dark Crusade.

Basically, by RTS launch standards it's pretty massive. The only limiting factor is that by 40k standards, having only four factions is a little thin, given how many there are in the game's universe. Four is perfectly standard for launch-version RTS games, but a few big 40k ones are missing here, most notably Chaos, the Astra Militarum, and the Eldar - who all featured prominently in the previous games. (The Astra Militarum are present and briefly playable in DoW 4's prologue, and pop up here and there in other missions as blobs of unfortunate cannon fodder, so you could reasonably hope they might arrive after launch. More factions have already been promised by King Art for 2027.)That scope is mirrored, in a way, by King Art's approach to Dawn of War 4's mechanical side. DoW has a little bit of every Dawn of War in it - yes, even Dawn of War 3, with its huge scale, spectacle, many active abilities, and Imperial Knight-class super units, for starters - and it's seemingly all the better for it. The sense when you play is that you're playing Dawn of War in its ultimate form: lavish, spectacular, dark, authentic, deep, and complex - but also a little campy, playful, and inviting to newer RTS players too.

Some more of that generosity, then: there are times, particularly when Orks and Admech are involved, where the screen is quite literally full of units. An ingenious simplifying factor in these cases is the new right-click-and-drag function, whereby you can select a group of enemies to attack in the same way you'd click-and-drag to choose your own units to command. Doing so sees your selected units automatically prioritise targets amongst the opposing group you've chosen.

You might think that sounds like it's oversimplifying the approach, removing the hard-earned skill of RTS veterans to micromanage, a bit like the combo shortcuts of modern fighting games. But that's not quite right. Instead, in practice I found it means you can efficiently move huge numbers of units into battle knowing they won't make truly irrational decisions (for the most part), and then get into micromanaging after the fact - which is where the great players will undoubtedly be separated from the good. The same goes for active abilities, of which there are quite a few in DoW 4: you can right-click on one to have that unit automatically use it when it comes off cooldown, so you know you're not leaving anything forgotten amongst the carnage.

"We basically said: let's give the player the option to automate a lot of things," Theysen explained to me. "It might not be 100 percent perfect - there is still something you can do if you really want to be more optimal - but you know, it's pretty good, right? And it's definitely better than not using something because you're overwhelmed." To Theysen, "the philosophy is that as a normal player, an average player, this really helps you out. If you're into your esports, and you're a really high-end RTS player, then you can probably still optimise things and you [will] want to do it yourself. But you know, not everybody is about 20 clicks-per-second."

I was curious about this - so many games with a PvP element find themselves falling into the trap of appealing to the competitive hardcore, leaving the often larger audience behind. "Basically, what we're saying is: we do not decide if this is, you know, the next big esports title. There are a lot of people with a lot more money who tried that," Theysen explained. The team is instead simply trying to "make sure there is no good reason why it couldn't be … but also, this is not our goal. We are not designing the game for the top one percent. It's more like, okay, we want a lot of people having fun with it, and we want to make sure that if people want to play it competitively, it's possible."The approach is the same when it comes to elements of balance - some of which, such as the reinforce function, have already become discussion points on certain subreddits. "If you balance everything for the top one percent then the game might feel wrong for the 99 percent. Because for them it's like: why are these units so weak? Well, they are so weak because there is some sort of thing that some pro player can maybe pull off."

On the topic of reinforcement, players can hit the reinforce button and have a squad produce a new unit after a short moment (or right-click it to have it automatically done as soon as resources are available). Theysen told me that, initially, the game didn't have on-field reinforcements at all. It's a returning feature from the first Dawn of War game, and quite a unique one. Later games from original developer Relic, such as Company of Heroes, saw reinforcement shift to be something only done on recalling your units to their main base. But at first it "felt a little bit weird - it's like lore-wise, it doesn't really make sense. And it's also not a super typical RTS feature."Then the team tested it just in case it was fun, "and it turned out to be really good because it's a way to bring the metagame, or the economy, into the battles, right?" Suddenly the player has a choice between spending lots of money to reinforce mid-battle, prolonging and maybe turning the battle in your favour, or they can micromanage more closely for a more efficient result. In practice I found myself having it set to 'on' almost by default - but in a way that's a conscious decision too, and a kind of crutch that I may have to ween myself off if I want to improve as a player over time. (Or, equally, something I can just leave on, so I can get on with having fun smashing my sea of Orks into Admech turrets.)

Even Dawn of War 4's headline visual innovation is a consideration here. The combat director feature - where hordes of individual units lock onto each other in melee with bespoke fighting animations - works in such a way as to take exactly the same amount of time as if your units were fighting each other the old-fashioned RTS way, Theysen assured me. "The things that are going on below the hood and the visualisation are completely separate from each other," he explained. "You could deactivate the whole thing and the game would still play exactly the same." Including it is essentially a flourish: something done, as I wrote when first testing it out, simply because one of King Art's animation specialists loved how it worked in the original and wanted to try something cool with it here.

This, and so much of Dawn of War 4's apparent munificence, brought another question to mind. How exactly can the team - not a particularly large one, by modern development standards - manage this approach? Amongst all the talk of ballooning budgets and sustainability in games these days, here's a fairly modest studio stacking feature on feature, promising lengthy post-release support - and, crucially, a lot of these little things are what you could fairly describe as luxuries.

Theysen put it down to a few things. The first is time: "We were very lucky," he said, with the team having worked on the game for around five years now. "That's a very long time." But that was made possible by the relatively small team size, "so the burn rate - the overall budget - is not higher than for other RTS games." The other factor is that they've been working with their current tech and fellow teammates for a long time, making that development more efficient. "Since we had the technology, had a lot of experience already, and had the time, we could basically say: okay, let's try to get it right - and everybody was very supportive," he added, citing publisher Plaion and Games Workshop's patience.

"If you look at some RTS games in the past, and you hear they had like an 18-month development time… I would say it's not possible to really make a great RTS game in 18 months," he continued. "Because it's just too complex - even if you have the technology, you discover stuff while you're working on the game, and then you can't follow up because whatever, you have to hit the next deadline. For us it was more like: we like the long development times. We also like long pre-productions where we can really figure out what we [want to] try and do here? And really, really experiment."

There's one more factor Theysen put the most emphasis on, however. "We really try hard to keep the team together. So here there are a lot of people who worked at King Art for like 15 years, or even longer, and everybody knows everybody in the team. It's like you have a sort of shorthand, but also you really understand, well, who is good at what." He gave an example: "Someone might not be the right person for something, and maybe if we put these two together, that's going to be great because you know this is really something they want to work on."

He cites the combat director again. "That was something that came from our head of animation, who was like a huge Dawn of War 1 fan, and he basically said: yeah, we can do the same thing, but we could also make it cooler; give me a couple weeks and I'll try to figure it out. And [our] first impression, everybody was like: I don't know how this is supposed to work. Because we have literally tens of thousands of permutations of who can fight who, right - because not only do we have almost 100 units, we also have all the different weapons and all of that stuff. But he basically said: 'No, no, I have a plan, let me try it out.'"

The result is a feature I've genuinely never seen before in the genre, amongst a sea of other neat little ideas that have no doubt come from someone wanting to try something cool, and insisting they have a plan.

"It basically comes down to having the time first, but also having the confidence," Theysen said. "And knowing the guy [so] that you say: okay, if he believes that works, it probably will, so let him try it. And I think this is really, really valuable. Because you cannot just buy a team. Or cannot just say, you know, we're doing the next great RTS or shooter or whatever it is, and now we buy everybody that we need for that and two years later we have a great game. That's not how it works."

All this has to be hedged by the fact I've only played a chunk of Dawn of War 4 so far, of course. But what a glorious, grim, bewitchingly good chunk it's been. The scale of some of my campaign battles has been stupendous, with huge numbers of units quite literally filling the screen and a level of visual spectacle rarely seen in this genre. The commitment to doing 40k right is eveident throughout, from the obvious narrative inspirations to the relentless, almost comical bleakness (especially in the level of violence imposed on those poor Astra Militarum). And the mix of older elements - requisition points, reinforcement, base-building - with more modern RTS intricacies feels considered and deliberate.

There'll be tinkering to do, no doubt - melee units look to be a bit too strong for now, and some better artillery-style disruptor units or abilities wouldn't go amiss - but there's time for that, and no doubt more to follow it after launch. And even as it is, this is one of the most impressive previews of a strategy game I have ever played.

#10626. New programmable photonic chip can control how fast light moves

Researchers at Seoul National University and the University of Seoul have developed a programmable photonic integrated circuit that can slow light whenever needed.

The team was led by Professors Namkyoo Park and Sunkyu Yu of the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at Seoul National University, working with Professor Xianji Piao of the School of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the University of Seoul.

Slowing Light Could Help Solve a Computing Bottleneck

The rapid growth of generative AI and large scale AI models has sharply increased the amount of computing power required by data centers and servers. Conventional electronic semiconductors are struggling to keep pace because they consume large amounts of energy and face limits in how quickly they can transmit data.

These challenges have intensified interest in optical computing, which uses light rather than electrical signals to process information. Optical systems could potentially move data at extremely high speeds while using less power.

However, light also presents a major challenge. Because it naturally moves at a fixed speed, it is difficult to delay optical signals or temporarily hold them in place. Those capabilities are essential for creating buffers and memory functions in optical computers.

To address this problem, the researchers designed a programmable photonic circuit that can control both the speed and shape of optical signals. Their approach provides more flexibility over "slow light" than previously proposed methods.

The study was published in the renowned international journal Advanced Science.

Why Optical Signals Sometimes Need To Wait

Photonic integrated circuits are emerging as a promising technology for processing information quickly and efficiently with light. In data centers, optical communication networks, and future computing systems, moving signals rapidly is only part of the challenge.

Systems must also ensure that different signals arrive at the correct time. In some cases, a light signal must be delayed so it can remain synchronized with other information moving through the system.

One method for creating these delays relies on coupled-resonator-induced transparency (CRIT), which uses interference among several optical resonators.

CRIT allows light within a selected frequency range to pass through a device while also reducing the speed at which the optical signal travels.

Traditional CRIT devices usually have operating characteristics that become permanent once they are manufactured. This makes it difficult to change how they function after fabrication.

For example, engineers who want to create a longer signal delay or work with a different frequency range often need to design and manufacture an entirely new photonic device.

That lack of adaptability increases the complexity of optical communication hardware and data center infrastructure. It can also raise costs and extend development schedules whenever new capabilities are needed.

The problem is especially important for AI servers and next-generation data centers, where enormous amounts of information must be processed in real time. Fixed optical components have therefore remained a major obstacle to more practical optical computing systems.

A Programmable Design for Controlling Light

The research team developed a different strategy by treating two optical states in CRIT systems, known as the bright mode and dark mode, as one unified degree of freedom. The researchers also added two controllable loop couplers.

Together, these changes created a new design principle for programmable photonic integrated circuits. Resonator arrangements that were previously locked into one configuration after fabrication could instead be adjusted for different purposes.

Using the new CRIT structure, the researchers showed that the movement of light could be delayed and controlled as needed. They also demonstrated that interference between the bright and dark modes could be handled as a single integrated design parameter.

This approach greatly expanded the flexibility of photonic resonator circuits that had previously been limited by fixed designs.

Controlling Delay, Bandwidth, and Signal Shape

The researchers theoretically demonstrated that the two loop couplers could be used to adjust the bandwidth and shape of the passband. They could also control how long signals were delayed and how efficiently those signals traveled through the circuit.

This means that both the speed and transmission behavior of optical signals could be reconfigured across entire systems containing multiple resonators, rather than only within a single resonator.

Numerical simulations also showed that the speed of optical pulses could be adjusted dynamically while the circuit was operating.

The results indicated that signal delay times could be changed without reducing processing performance. The system could also convert the frequency of light without requiring additional specialized components.

Simulations Suggest the Chip Could Be Practical

The researchers used three-dimensional electromagnetic simulations to test whether the CRIT device could be built on a silicon nitride (Si3N4) photonic integrated circuit platform.

They also evaluated a range of real-world issues that could affect the device during manufacturing and operation. These included material losses, differences in resonator quality, backscattering, coupling fluctuations, phase errors in the loop couplers, and thermal crosstalk.

The simulations indicated that the proposed structure could continue to operate reliably under realistic conditions.

One Chip Could Perform Several Optical Functions

The study introduces a programmable photonic platform that can control both the timing and frequency properties of light signals in real time.

The design could overcome the limitations of conventional optical delay devices, which typically perform only fixed functions. It also suggests that several important capabilities could eventually be combined within one photonic circuit.

These functions include signal synchronization, adjustable delay lines, optical buffers, and frequency conversion.

The same design principles may also be useful beyond CRIT systems. The researchers believe the approach could be applied to a broad range of photonic circuits based on resonators, potentially providing a foundation for more adaptable optical signal processing technologies.

Potential Benefits for AI and Data Centers

If the technology is commercialized, a single programmable optical chip could perform several tasks, including controlling signal speed and switching between different functions.

In that sense, the chip could operate in a way similar to a software-defined system, with its behavior adjusted according to changing needs.

This flexibility could help data centers and AI servers process information more efficiently while reducing energy consumption.

Combining several signal processing functions on one chip could also make optical communication equipment and sensor systems smaller and less expensive.

Over the longer term, the technology could support industries that depend on extremely fast information processing, including autonomous driving, next-generation communications, and quantum technologies.

Researchers Plan Larger Programmable Photonic Systems

Professor Namkyoo Park, co-corresponding author of the study from Seoul National University, stated, "This research is significant in that it proposes a new design principle that allows the flow of light within photonic integrated circuits to be reconfigured as needed, greatly enhancing design flexibility. We plan to expand this technology toward large-scale programmable photonic integrated circuits based on silicon photonics and photonic AI technologies."

Co-first authors Dr. Seungkyun Park and Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae, who led the theoretical framework and numerical analysis, added, "Through this study, we realized that reinterpreting conventional photonic resonator physics from a different perspective can serve as a starting point for discovering new functionalities in photonic integrated circuits. We plan to further develop this research toward practical device implementation and experimental validation."

Dr. Seungkyun Park is affiliated with the InnoCORE PICORE Center at KAIST and is currently conducting research on photonic AI and quantum optics at the Photonic Systems Laboratory, Seoul National University.

Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae is conducting research on programmable photonic integrated circuits at the Intelligent Wave Systems Laboratory, SNU.

The research received support from the Ministry of Science and ICT through the Innovative Research Center (IRC) program, the Basic Research Laboratory (BRL) program, and the Young Researcher Program.

Dr. Seungkyun Park also participated in the study with support from the InnoCORE program (PICORE Center).

Materials provided by Seoul National University College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
