#10624. MIT’s new lidar chip could give self-driving cars a wider view

Lidar technology uses pulses of infrared light to calculate distances and create detailed 3D maps of the surrounding environment. This allows autonomous vehicles to detect objects in their path and respond quickly. However, conventional lidar sensors are often large and costly, and many rely on moving components that can wear out over time. These limitations make the systems difficult to use in a wider range of settings.

MIT researchers have now developed an approach that could lead to smaller, more durable lidar sensors that operate without any moving parts. Their advance centers on a new silicon-photonics chip, a type of semiconductor device that controls light instead of electrical signals.

Existing lidar systems built with silicon-photonics chips usually have a narrow field of view. As a result, they struggle to scan areas located toward the edges of a scene. Previous attempts to expand this viewing range have often introduced extra noise and reduced measurement accuracy.

The MIT team addressed those problems by creating an array of integrated antennas that greatly limits unwanted crosstalk, which occurs when neighboring antennas interfere with one another. The design allows the chip to scan across a broader field of view while producing less noise than other silicon-photonics-based methods.

The advance could support the development of more capable lidar sensors for challenging uses, including autonomous vehicle navigation, aerial mapping, and the monitoring of construction sites.

"The functionality we demonstrated in this work solves a fundamental problem for integrated optical-phased-array technology, enabling future lidar sensors that can achieve significantly higher performance than we could demonstrate previously," says Jelena Notaros, the Robert J. Shillman Career Development Associate Professor of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS) at MIT, a member of the Research Laboratory of Electronics, and senior author of a paper on this innovation.

The study also includes lead author and EECS graduate student Henry Crawford-Eng, along with EECS graduate students Andres Garcia Coleto, Benjamin M. Mazur, Daniel M. DeSantis, and Tal Sneh. The findings were published recently in Nature Communications.

Many traditional lidar systems use a large rotating unit to direct light pulses across a scene. When the light strikes nearby objects, it reflects back toward the sensor. The returning signals provide the information needed to reconstruct a detailed map of the environment.

Silicon-photonics-based lidar works differently. Rather than rotating a mechanical device, it scans a beam of light electronically in several directions with a system known as an integrated optical phased array (OPA).

At the heart of an OPA is a group of integrated antennas. Each antenna contains tiny, regularly spaced variations along its length. These features, known as corrugations, cause light from an input source to scatter upward and out of the photonic chip.

Researchers can control the direction of the outgoing beam by changing the phase of the light sent to each antenna. Adjusting these phases changes the angle at which the array releases light, making it possible to steer the beam without moving any physical components.

Placing the antennas close together creates a serious obstacle. Neighboring antennas can couple with one another, scrambling the light they produce. Engineers have traditionally prevented this interference by increasing the distance between antennas, but wider spacing creates a different set of problems.

When antennas are too far apart, the array produces several copies of the same beam at different angles. The primary beam can only be moved a limited distance before it becomes difficult to distinguish from these additional copies.

"This limits our field of view, so the autonomous vehicle now only knows what is in front of it for a certain angular range," Garcia Coleto explains.

The unwanted beam copies, called grating lobes, can confuse the sensor and generate false detections. They also consume energy that could otherwise be directed into the main beam.

To overcome this tradeoff, the MIT researchers developed antennas with reduced crosstalk that can be positioned close together without strongly coupling.

In a conventional OPA, every antenna has an identical structure and uses the same pattern of corrugations. When these matching antennas are placed close together, they interact very strongly.

The MIT team instead created a repeating set of three antennas with distinct shapes. They changed the width of the antennas as well as the size and placement of the corrugations. Because the antennas have different geometries, each one also has a different propagation coefficient, which describes how light moves through the structure.

"Because the antennas have very different propagation coefficients, when we put them close together, essentially each antenna doesn't 'see' the antenna next to it. Therefore, it won't couple with its neighbor," Garcia Coleto says.

Making Different Antennas Behave the Same Way

Reducing the coupling was only part of the challenge. Although the antennas needed different propagation coefficients, they still had to release light in the same consistent manner.

The team designed the antennas around three essential requirements.

Each antenna had to emit the same amount of light. Every antenna also needed to release its beam at the same angle when receiving the same wavelength. Finally, the angle of emission had to change evenly across the entire array as the beam was steered.

"We have this challenge where we require the antennas to have different geometries to reduce the crosstalk, but we need to simultaneously design the antennas to have the same emission characteristics. While it is possible to engineer this, it is extremely difficult because, typically, when antennas are designed with different geometries, they tend to behave differently," Crawford-Eng says.

The researchers began by developing the basic electromagnetic theory describing how radiative modes couple. They then used this theoretical framework to guide the design and computer simulation of the antennas.

Based on those calculations, the team manufactured an OPA containing the reduced-crosstalk antennas. The antennas were placed much closer together than those in a conventional system, and the completed device was then tested experimentally.

Interference Falls From About 100 Percent to 1 Percent

Under the conditions of the experiment, a typical OPA would have produced coupling of approximately 100 percent. The MIT design lowered that coupling to about 1 percent while still generating one clean and precise beam.

The system accurately steered the beam across a broad field of view without producing any grating lobes. This combination of wide scanning, low interference, and strong beam quality addresses one of the central obstacles facing integrated lidar technology.

The researchers now plan to refine the method so the system can cover an even broader viewing range. They are also studying another possible approach to wide field-of-view performance that emerged while they were developing the underlying theory.

"This work addresses a longstanding challenge in integrated optical phased arrays: simultaneously achieving both a wide field of view, which requires dense antenna spacing, and high beam quality, which requires low crosstalk between neighboring antennas. The authors solve this problem with an elegant antenna design. Their innovation is an important step forward for chip-scale, solid-state beam-steering technology," says Joyce Poon, professor of electrical and computer engineering at the University of Toronto and director of the Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, who was not involved with this work.

The Semiconductor Research Corporation, the National Science Foundation, an MIT MathWorks Fellowship, the U.S. Department of War, and the MIT Rolf G. Locher Endowed Fellowship supported the research, in part.

Some of the work was carried out using MIT.nano facilities.

Materials provided by Massachusetts Institute of Technology. Original written by Adam Zewe. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10623. With a cracking new tactical feature and dedicated UI "strike team", Football Manager 27 is a huge step back towards FM's best form

Welcome back, Football Manager. I've only played half a day or so of it, whistling through my inductions, board meetings and opening transfer window, but already the positive signs are there. Football Manager 27 is a big, much-appreciated step in the right direction. In a way it's a step back - this is essentially the game I wish FM26 had been - but sometimes that's necessary. One back, two forward. At least it's genuine progress. This is a Football Manager game again.

If that "game I wish FM26 had been" line has raised an Ancelotti-eqsue eyebrow, here's what I mean by it: FM27 has got rid of most - if not quite all - of the cards-and-tiles UI that gave FM26 its notoriety. It's dramatically easier, faster, and more sensible to navigate, though I still think there are places to improve. But it's also maintained the best part of last year's entry, in the majorly overhauled in- and out-of-possession tactics systems. And it's built on that system smartly: elements are tinkered with and made clearer; other parts are expanded, and a new system added entirely, in something called "tactical effectiveness". This sounds almost comically dry but, in true Football Manager fashion, that's the good stuff. This is a really smart system, one that brings with it a huge boost to the role-playing side of the game. Combined with the rolling-back of various UI elements to more traditional FM fare, it makes me feel about as optimistic as I have been for this series for a little while.

It's worth focusing on last year's game for a moment. "We had a lot of technical challenges to solve," explains Rémy Boicherot, FM27's head of design, to me in an interview at Sports Interactive's head office in East London. Part of that was porting the interface to the new engine itself, with last year's game moving to Unity for the first time, and requiring that unprecedented year's delay as a result. The other was with "new graphical data," as Boicherot puts it. "It's a huge undertaking to transfer technology, and this is what we had to focus on last year to release the game." The studio's feedback rounds with players have since brought up two big points of improvement they hoped to make this year. The first, unsurprisingly, was the UI. The second, a little more interestingly, was the question of immersion. "The sense of feeling lost in that world."

With FM27, and those technical challenges seemingly now overcome, "we just focused on gameplay and making sure that the game resonated with our community," Boicherot continues. "We focused all our efforts into this. It was really a consolidation year. We still used a lot of these new philosophies to empower the interface, but with more of a familiar feel, and with just more efficiencies all throughout."

Ant Farley, senior features manager at Sports Interactive, put it similarly. "We were building from scratch," he said, which meant "everything needing to be built from the ground up, and just the volume of work that that entails and the technical complexity of that mammoth task. You can zero in on [the fact] that didn't give us much room to iterate."

He says the dev team, as a result, "realised that FM26 is a game that's good if you're very focused on your own team, and becomes [one with] quite an amount of friction when you're trying to be part of that wider world, starting to browse around." That realisation came "very early on," he says: "We really need to work on this, and the tiles and the remit of the tiles, I think is the really important significant change there… I think on paper the notion of a tile as being both navigation and information is really quite powerful. But what I think we did was, we relied on it for navigation, rather than it being a kind of secondary purpose for it. It became their primary purpose in a lot of places, and what that means then is, you're constructing screens in a way that imbalances the information to some extent."

The improvements are immediately evident. The navigation bar is more sensibly organised, the "portal" now more closely resembles your old inbox, with a bit of colour-coding thrown in. In the simplest terms, I found myself running down far fewer navigational cul-de-sacs than with FM26.

There are still snags, of course. Personally, I think the entire cards-and-tiles system should be left behind now, rather than just most of it. There are just very few circumstances under which a pop-up window is more useful or efficient than navigating to a full page. Boicherot's view here is that certain systems, such as messages from in-game people, suit the pop-up windows better - he may be right, though for me the two are essentially competing UIs overlaid on one another. The classic FM experience - not a spreadsheet, as people like to call it, but really a kind of web browser, where you can seamlessly move from your email inbox-cum-to-do-list to a series of interlinked wiki pages via hyperlinks and a clearly defined navigation bar - remains unbeaten. It's a single, intuitive and widely recognised visual language: underlined text takes you to a page on that topic, while dropdown menus and the nested ones within them take you to the most essential hub pages you'll visit most often. The tiles system only muddies the waters.

Thankfully the team, such as Boicherot, still seems to largely agree. "As a player, you don't want to think about the UI," he says. "Every time you watch a show, you don't want to learn how to use a [remote] control again. So with FM, we just wanted to get that familiar feel again." The studio had a "whole dedicated strike team" to resolving the issue, while the wider team, he says, has coupled that rework with the new drive for immersion, and in turn essentially adapted most of the compositions of the game's various screens to focus on "dynamic elements, on player stats, on the latest matches, who's hot, who's not, who's performing, underperforming. We have quite a lot of news stories as well. We wanted to make sure that we had better journalists in-game, reporting on more interesting stories, and basically giving you a better window to the world."

It's all good progress, ultimately, even if I still have my foibles with the card system's leftovers. And those new additions are really the things I want to talk about - and which I can tell Sports Interactive's developers are keen to talk about as well. Don your anoraks, then. It's time to talk about tactical effectiveness.

The system works like this: replacing the old and somewhat ignorable gauge for your team's tactical familiarity, there's now a gauge called tactical effectiveness. That gauge is influenced by four sub-categories: tactical understanding; player ability; player buy-in; and next match info. Those are then influenced by another layer of influence beneath them. Tactical understanding, for instance, depends on a combination of your player's understanding of their in-possession shape, passing style, build up, chance creation, out-of-possession shape, line of engagement, pressing intensity, and the pressing trap. You'll recognise most of those as tactical settings you can tinker with, which we'll come back to shortly. And even that layer, at times, has things that influence it again. Chance creation, for instance, is dictated by the training contribution, match exposure, players' tactical ability (which I understand from Sports Interactive developers to be influenced by their adaptability, teamwork and other attributes), and the tactical complexity of what you're asking them to do.

Sports Interactive says this is a result of direct conversations they've had with their connections in the Premier League and wider parts of the professional game. Fundamentally, Boicherot says, the previous tactical familiarity system was "quite simplistic" in comparison to what real managers were thinking about. "We also looked at Pep Guardiola's approach at City. [It] was very much, spend quite a lot of money on players, and then you don't see them play for quite a while until he knows that they're ready. And when he puts them on the pitch, they are effective. They just understand how to implement his vision directly on the pitch, and that was something that we felt we couldn't capture with the old system."

All of this is presented in a slightly underwhelming screen of several plain columns with a list of the above factors on them, which is a bit of a shame, but the impact it already had on my thinking in just a few hours with the game was huge. This is, ultimately, a role-playing mechanic, acting as a kind of bridge over the gap between how you think about tactics in Football Manager, and how a real manager might think about them in the real world. The biggest impact happens in how you think about the game - which, in a way, is where a lot of the playing of Football Manager really happens.

Beyond Boicherot's case of Pep's new players, an example given in a brief introduction was a good one: the "new manager bounce" - the notion that a team, after a previous, underperforming manager gets the sack, suddenly has a short-term improvement in results. Technically this is a bit of a myth; the data apparently suggests changing gaffers doesn't actually lead to a noticeable short-term uptick in results on average. But when it does happen, the theory is that these new managers tend to go "back to basics" with their tactics, simplifying instructions for bogged-down, low-morale players and letting them play their game with a bit more freedom.

Take Michael Carrick, for instance, from my trusty old management basket case Manchester United. Previous manager Ruben Amorim's system was complex and demanding, requiring both immense fitness and a large amount of tactical understanding and adherence from his players. Michael Carrick, then an interim, came in and seemingly reverted the team to something far simpler, and the short term impact was an immediate uptick in results. He put Bruno Fernandes back in the number 10 role, reverted to a familiar 4-2-3-1 formation, gave a relatively large amount of creative freedom to more creative players, and set them up in more of a fluid counterattacking mid-block as opposed to Amorim's structured, finely-tuned high press. And initially they did well! And now this season, after opponents had all summer to work through their opposition analysis and realise the tactical plan was actually quite simple, they've been "found out", as the football cliché goes - with results flipping into disaster territory.

FM27's tactical effectiveness system is designed to do something similar (although perhaps not quite as drastically as the Carrick disaster). Simple tactics, such as going long to a target forward, staying compact and hitting teams on the counter, are more easily grasped and implemented by players, leading to better short-term results. Complex pressing, inverting fullbacks, and intricate tiki-taka football meanwhile will likely take far longer to master, potentially leading to early-season struggles as the team learns your fancy, armchair-tactico system. And so in comes the role-playing: do I gamble on tactical purism for a higher long-term ceiling? Or do I secure my short-term future and attempt to gradually transform the team over time? Will fans accept my route one football, even if it gets me immediate results? Will players feel comfortable playing it?

This is where other, interlinking systems come in, and now my anorak is positively bristling. Training has also had quite a major rework, for instance. Training sessions have new categories: tactical understanding, match preparation, footballing development, and physical and recovery. Many of the sessions are familiar, but their recategorisation is important. Previously, we're told, sessions were primarily seen as a means of improving players' attributes - "Defending: Engaged" improved things like tackling and heading, for instance - while a few others helped with tactical familiarity or match fitness, without a clear gauge of their effectiveness.

Now, all of the sessions that come under "tactical understanding" will, you guessed it, help with the "tactical understanding" aspect of overall tactical effectiveness. And knowing that the gauge for, say, your team's understanding of your preferred passing style is in the orange will immediately indicate to you that you need to schedule a few more "Passing Style" training sessions. That then needs balancing with developmental sessions, match preparation sessions such as set pieces - now broken down into individual ones such as corners, free kicks, and so on - and recovery. It's another aspect influenced by actual conversations with those in the real game. Farley highlights the modern emphasis on "tactical periodisation," the approach of making training sessions resemble matches in various ways, down the certain "game state" such as being 1-0 down, consolidating a lead, and so on.

"It's going back a few years now," Farley continues, offering one example of how the team learned from the real game, "but we were very fortunate - I won't name the manager - we had a manager do a 'foot talk' for us, where they were talking about that tactical periodisation, and that kind of, almost playbook, American football-style of: now execute this plan. And that being something they would communicate from the touchline. And it was during covid, behind closed doors games, right? So then when I next watched this team play and you have the benefit of there being no crowd noise and everything, you really actually see it in action with that insight."

Beyond that, there's now an impact on your squad-building, too. Recruiting effectively now means considering your scout's take on a player's tactical suitability - are they likely to fit a high-pressing, high-technicality style such as Guardiola-like tiki-taka? Would James Tarkowski suit a high defensive line? Does Rayan Cherki want to play park-the-bus football? Bruno Fernandes, clearly sensing the importance of the topic with FM27's impending release, made his thoughts quite clear on what he thinks about not pressing opponents after Manchester United's humbling defeat to Como just the other week. This is the tactical effectiveness system personified.

"Take Thierry Henry moving from Arsenal to Barcelona," Boicherot suggests. "There was a big, big adoption time, right? But others are a bit more efficient with different types of player roles. It depends on their attributes, their stats, on their adaptability." It's essentially a melding of soft skill man-management with the modern, analytic side of the game - the type of blend that makes the very best coaches in the world so effective.

If you hadn't guessed, I'm a fan. It's a wonderful way to bring the type of gap-filling I'd already been doing with my imagined narratives into the reality of Football Manager itself. Beyond it, there are more tweaks that get the nod, too. Touchline shouts return, having been patched in apologetically to FM26, but now they're accompanied by a nifty feature that runs in tandem called Tweaks. These are basically one-touch tactical shortcuts you can instigate without leaving the in-match screen, shouts-style, to get your team to push up, or step off the press, or, much to my enjoyment, send the goalkeeper forward for set pieces. Notably Sports Interactive opted to make these tweaks stay active after you make them, until you consciously turn them back off again, as opposed to operating on a timer. The theory there being that the decision to turn them off is often as interesting as the decision to implement them in the first place.

"We wanted to capture a bit more of a 'duel' type of experience against the opposing team manager," Boicherot explains. "How do you want to disrupt your opposition in a quick way, really trying to capture the kind of quick instructions you tell your captain on the sideline and let them go and implement."

International management also gets a welcome bit of attention, with training now properly implemented for that, as does the 2D engine, which has had a lovely visual revamp that still maintains a pleasingly retro tinge. There are reworks to how players' running speed works, which interlinks with fatigue and even the types of runs they might attempt. Tied to that is a new instruction for how a forward should attack the box, all this together theoretically giving slower forwards greater influence on the game. And the onboarding experience has been tinkered with once again, with more manager customisation options and tweaks to how your traits and attributes are set up.

The headline changes, though, are where FM27 is at its best so far. It is easier to play and navigate, "faster to the fun," as well-trained UX developers often like to say, and has genuine potential to be the most tactically interesting and nuanced game in the series to date. Shuffle away the last of those remaining cards, and this could be a seriously good year. Thank goodness for that.

#10621. Mouthwashing is finally on consoles and Xbox Game Pass, and it's a Halloween must for horror fans

A mutilated man stares at you, his single piercing eye following as you move around the room. His filthy bandages hide a ruined face, raw lips circling disturbingly perfect teeth, and his moans echo through the halls. But that eye: it knows something you don't. At least not yet.

Developed by Wrong Organ, Mouthwashing rightly earned a lot of praise when it was released in 2024; it's an extraordinary first-person horror adventure game placing you among the five-strong crew of the Tulpar, a commercial space freighter part-way through its year-long mission to carry its unknown cargo across the stars. Moments into its dramatic opening scene - "0 days before the crash", we're told - things go dramatically awry, establishing a dire tone and a baseline of dread that only builds from there.

Mouthwashing isn't a game laden with jumpscares; instead it is a masterfully crafted discomfort simulator, conjuring unease through its claustrophobic environmental design, strong audio work, disorientating structure and pacing, and a lo-fi visual style that creates spaces for your imagination to fill.

Slowly, for instance, the Tulpar's already gloomy corridors constrict, shrunk by the crew's increasingly desperate use of repair foam; this continuous degradation of the space around you mirroring the minds of its characters, as circumstances grow increasingly dire. For as enjoyable as Mouthwashing's light puzzling is, you're really here for its unexpectedly impactful story.

This is an intelligent game that trusts its audience, and that's most obvious in its breathless, non-chronological narrative. It's a story that doesn't stay still, that offers no moment to breathe. Scenes transition into each other with a digital datamoshing effect, almost as if you're watching system files recovered long after the fact. And each transition often signals a different, disorientating kind of switch, as it's not always clear when - or even who - you are now.

I don't want to spoil much, but there's a surprising amount of subtlety to the way characters are portrayed and gradually evolve. One crew member, for instance, sleeps near a statue of the shipping company's cartoon mascot, which emits voice lines whenever someone draws near. The reason why isn't spelled out to you, but on a second playthrough, this small act of self-preservation becomes clear. And Mouthwashing only grows more rewarding with each subsequent return as you start to notice this attention to detail more and more.

Speaking of characters, one is particularly deplorable; a truly detestable creature up there with Shepherd from the original Modern Warfare trilogy and perhaps even Eric Sparrow if you're a Tony Hawk's Underground fan. Their narcissist journey, culminating in a spiral of delusion that really made my skin crawl, exemplifies what makes Mouthwashing work so well.

Its horror stems from the psychological disintegration of the Tulpar's crew, and the way the resulting violence unfolds. Violence is sparse and unglorified here, but you're often an active participant, much of the nastiest actions occurring when you're in direct control. It absolutely made me wince a few times.

And then comes the closer; one of the most moving monologues I've heard in a video game. It's a final message - not only to a member of the crew, but to you the player - on taking responsibility. By this point, what's done is done, leaving one - perhaps futile - final decision to make. One last chance for a kind of salvation. For me, it's a message that hit home.

Mouthwashing is powerful stuff, then; a different sort of horror this Halloween. It's an easy recommendation, and has been wowing PC players for a while. As of today, though, it's finally available for consoles - PS4, PS5, Switch, and Xbox Series X/S to be exact - and is out on Game Pass too. Additionally, there's currently 40 percent off on Steam.

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

#10620. Will Galaxy S27 Ultra and Pixel 12 mark the end of the flagship era of smartphones?

Like most Android fans, you probably enjoy having the latest and greatest smartphone buried in your pocket. The best camera, the best display, the fastest processor, and of course, as much RAM as any given smartphone OEM will pack in. And even as we’ve watched the typical flagship price balloon into the four-digit space, trade-in offers, carrier deals, and steep sales on certain hardware brands like Pixel have made it all too easy to keep up on what’s new in gadgets without breaking the bank year-in and year-out.

Obviously, all of those routes towards a cheaper smartphone purchase still exist today — I assure you, anyone who regularly tunes into the NFL can recite Verizon, T-Mobile, and AT&T’s various deals by heart. But prices are rising so rapidly, with no sign of slowdown in sight, that I can no longer be sure any of those routes towards making new hardware more expensive will be available tomorrow. And honestly, we might hit a point where it’s increasingly difficult to recommend these phones to all but the most hardcore of enthusiasts, no matter how many sweeteners you add to the deal.

Throughout 2026, we’ve watched as new generations of barely-modified hardware have launched with $100 price hikes. We’ve watched as months-old hardware suddenly jumped up in price without so much in the way of a warning or an official announcement. And even outside of smartphones, you have to wonder if Google really hoped its latest lineup of laptops would’ve launched with more models available for under four figures. What’s been an underlying problem throughout the market throughout the last year, though, seems ready to burst into something much larger beginning throughout the next few months.

Recent price leaks have not exactly filled me with confidence over what hardware to recommend to anyone in the market for a new phone. The Galaxy S27 Ultra is sounding like it could arrive starting at $1,500, $100 more than the S26 Ultra’s current MSRP and $200 more than what that phone launched at earlier this year. Motorola’s attempt to come back to the flagship space in the US for the first time since 2023 could be completely derailed by rumors of an even higher price point that guarantees it won’t appeal to anyone. And while we’re certainly too far out to really predict anything regarding Google’s next-gen Pixel pricing, if you think the Pixel 12 might be your next phone, I’d start saving today.

As you climb towards the $2,000 mark, it’s worth putting these costs into perspective. These are laptop prices — maybe not that new “PROLED” MacBook Pro, but certainly other upper-high end laptops from companies like Apple, Dell, and HP. $1,500 will buy you an mid-tier home treadmill, or a nice OLED TV, to say nothing of the truly practical ways you can put that much cash to good use in a time where the costs of everything is rising. While some might think there is no price ceiling that truly keeps them away from new Android hardware, I’d be willing to bet plenty of potential buyers are already being priced out of the high-end market entirely. We’re reaching a breaking point where the question might not even be about whether you can afford something, but whether you should, leaving customers with just two routes forward: keep your existing device, or opt for a midrange option when you can.

Sure, saying “no thanks” to something like the Pixel 11 Pro XL right now might be easy enough. But moving forward, practically every single Android user will need to set some kind of personal limit on the price of a new smartphone, and if you’re convinced the midrange space might serve as your sole savior, you might want to think again. While RAM prices are bound to affect the final cost of Google’s Pixel 12, those constraints on a final bill of materials might mean a whole lot more to something like the Pixel 11a. After all, when you’ve already seen the Pixel 10a and its generation-old Tensor G4 chipset get hit with a $100 price bump, you have to wonder if it’s time to cut back on certain “premium” features.

The next few years of budget-oriented smartphones — and I really am talking about anything outside of the flagship space — is about to shift in a way we haven’t seen in over a decade. In the 2010s, cheap components and streamlined production costs helped bring about excellent midrange options like Google’s Pixel A-series, Samsung’s Galaxy A-series, and some select Moto G entries. Moving forward, these same companies will need to pick between higher prices and reduced spec sheets. The dream of yesterday’s flagship becoming tomorrow’s budget phone is, at least for the time being, dead and buried.

This isn’t just rampant speculation on my part. We’re already hearing about some companies making the decision to reduce RAM in their devices down to 8GB, 6GB, or even as low as 4GB, depending on the model and market it’s entering. It’s easy to see storage options also getting hit with their own downgrade — 128GB entry-level flagships could reemerge just as soon as they began to die off, and I wouldn’t be surprised to see 64GB options begin to reappear in sub-$600 models. That might be the sacrifice needed to keep other high-end features, like 120Hz OLED displays, hanging around in this price range.

I think after rebates, credits to Google One and AI Pro, my Pixel 11 Pro 512GB came out to ~$350, which would be amongst the smallest of all my "entertainment" expenses, and this is a tool I use multiple times a day that saves me time and money, also. I don't mind spending that money, and I don't see that stopping anytime soon. The only real issue is I wanted 16G of RAM on my device, and for that I spent more than I was expecting, but I'm pretty sure next year's trade-in will cover the additional expense.

I choose Pixel for the rapid updates, and Android, primarily. I can't get that with any other manufacturer except maybe sometimes Samsung.

I recognize I'm an outlier, my partner still runs a OnePlus 12r and is quite happy with it, and doesn't ever consider new phones unless it breaks.

If AI isn't making Google the money it needs for its stock to stay upwards, eventually things will need to change. Either phone prices/RAM comes down, or AI provides such a great life-changing experience (so far it definitely hasn't,) that I put my money elsewhere.

Of course, you can’t simply opt to forego RAM or storage in the same way that you can downgrade the display, and with the memory crunch set to possibly extend to 2030 or beyond, there could come a time where cutting back on raw specs or non-essential features stops being an either-or question. The knock-on effect is obvious: If you suddenly have a lot more people buying devices with less horsepower than previous generations, you’re going to see a reciprocal slowdown of any software development that requires hardware capable of reaching a certain threshold. Widespread on-device AI, for those of you who care about local models, seems like a pipedream at this point, but these market conditions are just as likely to crush, say, any hope of more games with high-end graphics coming to Android.

It’s not that the hardware won’t exist to support these features — flagships will obviously still exist even if their price tags balloon over the $2,000 mark. It’s the audience size you have to worry about; for a large majority of developers, it simply won’t make sense to continue pushing the envelope for what constitutes a smaller and smaller crowd of potential users.

The other solution is to hold onto whatever device you have in your possession right now, for as long as you can. That might not sound particularly exciting — I know I couldn’t tell you the last time I went more than six months without changing phones, even discounting review units — but it’s also the most reasonable approach to the current crisis. It’s not too late to jump on a recent flagship (think 2024 or later) before things get particularly dire, either, and with holiday sales coming up, I’d urge anyone on the fence about upgrading their current device with the means to do so to pull the trigger. Tomorrow’s price, simply put, will not be today’s.

And hey, if there’s a silver lining to be found here, I think it’s this: we’ve seeing tech companies across the board — hardware and software alike — grow increasingly comfortable with the idea that, for the user, resources can be effectively unlimited. Those days are gone, and it’s going to force software developers, including Google, to make some changes that are probably long, long overdue. We might lose out on big hardware improvements, resource-heavy advancements, and flagship-esque affordable smartphones, but there’s always a chance Chrome’s memory usage finally gets under control. It might not be worth all of this pain, but it’s something, right?

#10618. World’s first superconducting quantum heat engine could help unlock massive quantum computers

A newly developed superconducting quantum heat engine could deepen our understanding of thermodynamics while helping advance technologies needed for quantum computers with very large numbers of qubits.

Scientists are getting a clearer picture of how thermodynamics behaves in the quantum world, and that progress could benefit both quantum technology and our understanding of familiar thermodynamic principles. Researchers at Aalto University have now taken an important step by demonstrating the first cyclic quantum heat engine built inside a superconducting circuit.

The experiment connects two areas of physics that normally describe very different scales. Quantum mechanics explains the behavior of matter at extremely small scales, even below the size of atoms, while thermodynamics describes how heat and energy behave in much larger systems, from collections of molecules to the universe itself. Bringing the two together raises a fundamental question: what happens to familiar thermodynamic processes when quantum effects such as tunneling, entanglement, and superposition enter the picture?

A Heat Engine Built for the Quantum World

Conventional heat engines turn heat into useful work. James Watt's steam engine is one famous example, but the same basic concept remains central to modern transportation and electricity production, powering cars, ships, planes and many power plants.

The researchers have now created the world's first superconducting quantum heat engine. The extremely small device combines a transmon qubit, a resonator and a quantum refrigerator.

Operating under ultracold quantum conditions, the engine was able to use the tiny amount of available heat to repeatedly produce positive work. Achieving this kind of cyclic operation has been an important objective for researchers working on quantum heat engines. The result provides a proof of concept for superconducting heat engines that could eventually contribute to improved quantum computing technology.

The study, led by Academy Professor Mikko Möttönen, was published in Nature Communications.

Recreating an Otto Cycle Near Absolute Zero

To make the engine operate, the researchers reproduced an Otto cycle inside a superconducting circuit. The Otto cycle is a thermodynamic process also used in car engines and other conventional machines.

"In our experiment, we built a nanofabricated heat engine using superconducting circuits and operated it in a cryostat near absolute zero. At its heart is a transmon qubit, one of the basic building blocks of modern quantum technologies," says Tuomas Uusnäkki, the study's first author.

The researchers connected the transmon qubit to a quantum circuit refrigerator, allowing them to control heat flow on the quantum scale and demonstrate that this heat could be transformed into measurable work. A conventional heat engine normally relies on separate hot and cold environments. In this system, however, the same quantum refrigerator can supply both heating and cooling.

"Our quantum-circuit refrigerator can be tuned to both heat and cool the qubit on demand. Using carefully timed control pulses, we drove the engine in an Otto cycle and monitored the qubit state as the engine ran," explains Uusnäkki.

Measurements showed that heat passing through the qubit during the cycle was producing positive work.

"This is the first experimental demonstration of a cyclic quantum heat engine in superconducting circuits. Using a single controllable quantum refrigerator as both the hot and cold environment of the engine makes it simpler and more versatile," says Uusnäkki.

Toward Autonomous Quantum Computer Hardware

The researchers are now trying to improve the design and eventually develop a fully autonomous heat engine. One possible use would be reading out qubits without having to carry a microwave pulse from millikelvin temperatures all the way to room temperature.

That capability could become especially valuable as quantum computers grow. Autonomous devices integrated directly into superconducting circuits could reduce both the cost and complexity of machines containing very large numbers of qubits.

"Finland's Quantum Technology Strategy envisions a quantum computer with one thousand logical qubits by 2035, which probably means hundreds of thousands of physical qubits. Doing that with current technology requires millions of microwave cables costing thousand euros each. The cables also introduce noise into the system. Using autonomous devices instead would mostly eliminate the need for those cables," Möttönen says.

Reducing the need for those microwave connections could therefore address two challenges at once: the enormous hardware requirements of large quantum computers and the unwanted noise that cables can introduce into quantum systems.

The pioneering experiment was carried out using OtaNano, Finland's national research infrastructure for nano, micro and quantum technology. Funding came from the Research Council of Finland and the Finnish Cultural Foundation.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10617. How Applicant Tracking Systems Read Resumes: 7 Checks Developers Should Make

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A resume that looks perfect on screen can look like a pile of scrambled text to a program. Applicant tracking systems (ATS) and hiring tools do not see your layout. They extract text from a file, guess where the sections are, and store what they find. If that step fails, the best experience in the world never reaches a human reader.

This matters whether you are sending your own resume or building a tool that processes thousands of them. This article walks through seven checks you can make with a few lines of Python, including one script you can run today.

A resume is a document with no guaranteed structure. One person writes "Work History", another writes "Experience", and a third uses a two-column layout that reads out of order. Any tool that processes resumes first has to turn this into predictable fields: contact details, sections, dates and skills.

Thinking in terms of a target schema helps. SitePoint's article on JSON schema design for document AI shows how the structure of the labels you define determines whether a document pipeline works. The same idea applies here: decide which fields you need before you write any extraction code.

Do not judge a resume by how it looks. Judge it by what comes out when you extract the text. SitePoint's walkthrough on building a JSON dataset from PDF documents describes a pipeline that extracts text from PDFs and then cleans up common extraction artifacts. That cleanup step exists because raw extraction is rarely tidy. Read the raw output first, and note what is broken.

PDF files store where characters are placed on a page, not what they mean. The pypdf documentation on extracting text from a PDF says this directly: there is no semantic layer, spacing is hard to get right because text is absolutely positioned, and pypdf is not OCR software, so it will never extract text from images.

Two practical results follow. A resume saved as a scanned image has no extractable text at all. And fancy layouts such as columns, text boxes and tables can come out in the wrong order.

Once you can extract text, a short script can catch the common failures. This one reads a PDF and reports whether it contains text, whether an email address is found, and which standard sections are missing:

Run it on a resume and look at the result. If has_text is false, the file is probably an image. If sections are missing, check whether your headings use unusual names. A passing result does not prove that every ATS will read the file the same way, but a failing result is a clear warning.

Different tools parse differently, so one script is only one opinion. A resume writing service such as ResumeCroc lists a free ATS resume checker among its tools, which is a quick way to see how another tool reads the same file. The linked page is a commercial service page, and every ATS behaves differently, so treat any checker as one data point and compare it with your own extraction results.

Most extraction problems come from layout. Single-column pages, standard headings, plain fonts and real text instead of images give parsers the best chance. Avoid putting contact details in headers or footers, since some tools skip them. If you are building a parser, test files with columns and tables on purpose so you know how your tool fails.

Create a folder of sample resumes: a clean one, a two-column one, a scanned one and one with unusual headings. Run your check against all of them whenever you change your parsing code. This turns "it seems to work" into something you can verify, and it makes regressions obvious.

Do all applicant tracking systems read PDFs the same way? No. Parsers differ in how they handle layout, headers and tables, so test more than one file format and more than one tool.

Is a Word file safer than a PDF? Not necessarily. Both can parse well or badly depending on layout. Test your own file instead of assuming.

Can a script guarantee my resume will pass an ATS? No. A script can find obvious problems, such as missing text or headings, but each hiring tool has its own rules.

Hiring tools read text, not layout. Define the fields you care about, extract the raw text first, understand what PDF extraction cannot do, automate basic checks, compare with an outside checker, keep formatting simple, and test against a small set of sample files. The script above is short, but it moves the question from "does it look right" to "what does a program actually see".

#10616. Call of Duty Modern Warfare 4 review

It's easy to dislike Call of Duty. Aside from the annual cycle of development, marketing, and controversy that has defined the series identity for the past 22 years, the series has become an integral part of how video games prop up the military-industrial complex. Whilst some of the stories - especially 2025's Black Ops 7 - have deteriorated into the realms of science-fiction absurdity, many of the narratives you'll find in the Modern Warfare splinter of the series are more grounded in reality. When you're playing as semi-fictional UK and US troopers, taking part in illegal operations in foreign territory, wantonly executing people over differences in ideology, Call of Duty can feel gross: paper-thin propaganda designed to glorify war and reinforce lines between 'them' and 'us'.

But Modern Warfare 4 shies away from this. A little. The bar is low, granted, but there is a slightly more humanitarian slant to the game this year - aping 2009's No Russian, there are a few missions that plant you into the shoes of the 'enemy', and ask you to make moral choices that, I won't lie, I was rather shocked to see manifest in a CoD game. In 2019, developer Infinity Ward asked what would happen if there was a terrorist attack on the streets of London. In 2022, the studio ponders what happens when a co-ordinated international military operation goes awry on a Mexican oil rig. In 2023, we go to Lower Manhattan as the Russian army stages an attack on the New York Stock Exchange. So, in moving our lens and making us play as a North Korean militiaman, I was genuinely surprised to learn that - if you refuse your orders - you are summarily shot, leaving the rest of the mission to play out in front of your lifeless corpse. There is no 'game over' state for not complying: the horrors carried out by the ruthless regime carry on even in the face of your dereliction of duty. It might be using baby speak, by literary standards, but here I think Call of Duty is trying to make a point.

And it doesn't fall on ears unwilling to listen, either. Just this week, a South Korean politician urged the country's president to play Modern Warfare 4, so striking is its depiction of a theorised North Korean assault on the nation (which makes the dev's inclusion of real disaster footage an even more regrettable gaffe. Whilst it's important to remember that a country's population is not its leadership, the news has been full of North Korean threats and bluster for many years now. And in Modern Warfare 4, Infinity Ward wanted to tell a story "ripped from the headlines". The result is a slightly more 'reactive' setup, where a militarised nation responds to an invasion rather than instigates one, which in turn gives a sense of urgency and speculation that is more agreeable than, say, another shadowy skirmish in the Middle East, or an insurgency into some proxy state secretly controlled by Russia (don't hold your breath).

As we flip back and forth between ex-SAS hard bastard, John Price (who we've been following since 2007) and a ragtag bunch of conscribed South Korean soldiers and US Marine allies that find themselves in the middle of a brutal, casualty-heavy North Korean invasion, we get to see Call of Duty campaign design at its finest. There's a rousing John Wick-inspired shootout in a club that evolves into a parkour dash across Manhattan rooftops. There's a cinematic and genuinely impressive shooting gallery-like encounter in a trainyard that evokes classic arcade lightgun design. There's an expansive open world area that toys with stealth and sniping, there's a car chase through the streets of Paris, and there's a beachhead assault that ties it all together nicely.

Beyond even those, the standout mission is Stranded, which sees the South Korean team deep in enemy territory, fighting for their lives. This introduces crafting for a few missions - something that could feel tacked-on and rote, but is actually deployed like a lever by Infinity Ward to wedge open just how important each of the various tools at your disposal are. Want a thermal scope? Then you're going to prioritise that over more munitions. Want explosives? Then your basic arms will suffer. It serves to emphasise just how well every aspect of Modern Warfare's toolkit complements every other piece, resulting in this rich, deep, interlocking marvel that makes older Call of Duty games look rudimentary in comparison.

This is complemented by setpieces like Traffic, where you engage on a breakneck chase through the streets of Paris in a romp sure to cost the Parisian treasury millions in collateral damage. Here, you get to see the breadth and depth of the enemy AI that Infinity Ward has crafted - which no doubt went hand-in-hand with the new DMZ mode, more on that later - as we witness just how complex the studio's engine can get: destruction, cover mechanics, and so many different kinds of visual effect all rush on-screen at once. It's overwhelming, it's intimidating, it's electrifying. It's peak Call of Duty.

Between these standout missions, there are moments where you're asked to consider what it's like for the pawns on the board - the other soldiers, the unnamed, those whose strings are being pulled by forces they don't understand. It's a bit Fisher-Price, a bit A-Level, a 'War is Bad' pin badge worn on a jacket from an Army Surplus store, but hey: I'd rather this than a completely unobserved 'ooh-rah!' story about the US army coming to the rescue again.

The downside of the campaign comes more from its setup and pacing than its content, then. Price's story wraps up without applause, really - I won't ruin the specifics here, but his actions and what he does to quash the machinations of a very oddly-implemented Mads Mikkelsen are sort of left in the dark to rot, as the desperate resistance of our South Korean recruits takes the spotlight. Again, I think Call of Duty might be trying to say something here: that the morally ambiguous and collateral damage-accumulating actions of one desperate operative that actually saved the world don't get to breathe in the daylight; instead, the 'courageous' defense of privates on the frontline should be lauded. War is messy, no-one is in the right, everyone loses except for the multinationals putting the hardware in the hands of the humans. Is that what I'm supposed to come out of this thinking?

Whatever the emotional impact is, you can't argue with the physical one: this is a tight, eight-hour campaign with moments up there with Modern Warfare's best. It stops before it ever risks getting overlong, it's got plenty of new ideas to throw at you and - played on the hardest mode - it's a tight puzzle box of encounter design that's just as obsessed with detail as it is with Michael Bay-esque spectacle. It's a far cry from whatever the hell was going on in Black Ops 7 last year.

Reeling from the fact I think I nearly felt something during a Call of Duty campaign, I needed to boot up the multiplayer - a mode which has by now been refined into its near-perfect form, an ultra-fast, turbo-injected mosh pit of quick scopes, slide cancels, and kill corridors. Map design is great - of course it is, it's Infinity Ward - and the gunplay is good as it ever has been in the Modern Warfare games. Since 2019, when Infinity Ward went to great expense to rebuild its engine, the Call of Duty experience has been luxurious. The sound design alone is worthy of applause, every clack of a bolt-action lever, every shell casing falling to the floor, every fizz of thermite boring through your cover… it's immediately identifiable and extremely important. To play Modern Warfare 4 without a decent set of headphones or a good surround sound setup is to intentionally handicap yourself.

There are a few qualms, as ever: some respawn mechanics are vexing (especially on quicker, gnarlier maps - this week's addition of Shipment is a hilariously bad example of this), and some of the weapon balancing has yet to be totally calmed down, in part owing to a last-minute change to weapon damage that extends the time-to-kill beyond Infinity Ward's usual fare. Whilst this means there's a bit of chaos for early adopters like myself, it actually speaks to the level of nuance Infinity Ward has to play with: I'd love to see the backend of Modern Warfare 4, with all its knobs and dials, seeing how the studio tweaks and balances guns to keep up with what players are doing. The slightly lower time-to-kill is actually a fascinating experiment in figuring out what Modern Warfare is beyond its selection of guns - and I'd happily take a bit of weird balancing a deeper, more intricate damage system.

Speaking of, the customisation system also works nicely here. With the right attachments, some guns are elevated from 'useful' to 'essential'. The ISO Nightshade SMG seems a bit busted right now, and I keep getting chewed up by suspiciously powerful one-two taps of the Type 73 LMG. I'm a born sharpshooter, and the Oris 8.6 is an early favourite for popping heads across the map, especially dressed up with a 2.1x scope with thermal imagine. The overall economy of guns and range-versus-power feels a bit off as it stands - and is quite literally being addressed as I type - but I'm playing multiplayer in launch week, so there's always going to be some friction.

Then there's Kill Block: an experimental and dynamic rotating multiplayer map and mode concept. It's absurd in its pitch: by using a selection of 'slabs', it can form 500+ unique combinations of map, meaning no match plays out quite like the last. Maps in Call of Duty are (in)famously defined by their sight lines; if you stand here, you can shoot someone there. If you camp this corner, you'll rack up kills. If you snipe on this lane, you'll collect greenhorn scalps a-plenty. Kill Block turns all that on its head, forcing players to be more reactive, and engage more with the systems on offer in Modern Warfare 4, rather than rely on instincts baked in from two-plus decades of CoD. With the promise of more modular elements to come - from fan-favourite maps like Crash, for instance - I can see this mode and setup becoming yet another staple in the ever-expanding Call of Duty canon. This is how you breathe life into the more competitive-focused modes that often fail to shine alongside respawn-focused experiences like Team Deathmatch.

I have to praise the complexity and wizardry Infinity Ward has displayed with how the guns handle; each attachment is meaningful, most guns feel unique, and the feedback you get from firing any armament is exquisite. It has all the production value of a dedicated military sim, but in action it's readable and almost arcade-like in its simplicity. No one does it like Infinity Ward, and even though this feels like an iteration on the last three Modern Warfare games, I won't knock the developers down for staying in their lane and developing a formula that works.

And that brings us to DMZ. Initially, I was cynical. 'This is Call of Duty chasing the bag', I thought. 'This is trend-chasing gone awry'. But Infinity Ward is not afraid of change, and I shouldn't be either. The extraction shooter trend shows no signs of abating, and with even Call of Duty managing to make it work within the specific arcade-cum-sim setup of its engine, I need to accept that. I don't think it's a stretch to say DMZ could be a standalone game - packed with enough to go toe-to-toe with the likes of Marathon or Arc Raiders.

Simplified controls, motion sensor functionality, deadzone/trigger customisation, vibration/trigger effects, reduce tinnitus sound, audio focus (PS5 only), mono audio options, HUD customisation, motion blur/DoF customisation, colour filters, camera movement reduction options, aim assist, aiming sensitivity, movement/sprint assists, FoV sliders.

The Hajin city map, smartly repurposed from the campaign, is expansive and packed with nuance, and the tasks you're given within the city limits are quick and painless - more than I can say of Marathon. You can easily and somewhat permanently get cash that persists even if you die, you can sacrifice max health to 'revive' from a down-but-not-out state, and you get XP for doing just about anything. The result is an extraction shooter experience that feels more casual and agreeable to the average FPS enjoyer; this isn't a 'lifestyle game' like other extraction shooters. Even if it does have its own pseudo-stealth system, for some reason.

In many ways, DMZ emulates what Activision did with Warzone: a wrangling of a complicated formula that makes sense within the uber-specific Call of Duty ecosystem. It's a great interpretation of the PvPvE formula that's been slowly but surely evolving over the past decade or so, and I think this is where DMZ finds its niche: Arc Raiders has become more about the PvE side of things (at least in my experience) and Marathon and Tarkov go hard on the PvP. DMZ somehow treads the line between both and, as time goes on and Infinity Ward monitors the mode and how people engage with it, I imagine its niche will only become more distinct compared to its genre stable-mates. We're already seeing some changes bleed into the game, and it's not even been out a week - it bodes well for the future.

Modern Warfare 4 represents the highest tide for Call of Duty in seven years. Infinity Ward needed to pull off a balancing act I don't envy; injecting just enough nostalgia into a series propped up with so many conventions and expectations there's barely room for anything new, alongside enough innovation and intrigue to keep people interested. Beyond that, after several weaker years in a row, Black Ops 7 in particular caused enough reputation damage that even the most loyal CoD fanatics started to see through the series' aging veneer. And so, even if the campaign occasionally struggles under the weight of legacy, and the multiplayer is happy to keep rolling on the same wheels Modern Warfare has been using for four games now, Infinity Ward has succeeded - after four years away from the helm - in saving Call of Duty. Modern Warfare 4 is a course correction sorely needed, and deftly deployed.

A copy of the game was provided for this review by Activision Blizzard.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10614. 8-bit horror Tenebris Somnia mixes weird live-action horror with a retro 2D adventure to impressive and unsettling effect

I've been captivated by the idea of wrong-footing an audience ever since playing Faith: The Unholy Trinity. That game presents like an 8-bit horror, then proceeds to bend the rules of what an 8-bit game can do - or perhaps what you remember they could do - in order to surprise and scare you. Tenebris Somnia is very similar. Indeed, it's executive produced by the developer of Faith, Airdorf Games, so the connection runs even deeper. Tenebris Somnia utilises the same retro aesthetic with its grinding chiptune music, its pixelated graphics, and old-school presentation. But the difference is it takes the rule-bending a step further, essentially welding two very different experiences together: a retro-styled 2D adventure and a full-on live-action film. And it's horrific, in all the right ways.

To begin with, Tenebris Somnia is a slow, plodding, fairly unremarkable affair that plays a bit like a point-and-click adventure. You're a young woman on her way to her former boyfriend's apartment, checking it out because you suspect something has happened there. You move from screen to screen, examining points of interest as you pass them, collecting odds and ends - an empty bottle, a mysterious book - and storing them in your inventory for later. Eventually, there's a locked door, and you'll have to piece together the scattered clues in order to unlock it. And despite the apparent urgency of the situation - the carnage you find in your ex-boyfriend's apartment - the pace is slow; deliberately old-school in its approach. Walk back and forth, examine everything, try "combining" items in your inventory, "use" them on the environment. Did it work? No? Try something else.

But this is where Tenebris Somnia begins to distinguish itself: your busywork is often rewarded with a glossy live-action sequence - clips of real actors in impressive costume and make-up enacting key moments from the unfolding adventure. A person with what appears to be two faces, one inverted, rushes out of the darkness toward you. A strange woman suddenly appears in a lift, offering you a gift. Two waxen figures embrace each other in bed. A giant lady with a hole in her face stretches towards the ceiling. It's captivatingly weird and very well-produced, finished with a level of polish that's impressive in an otherwise lo-fi indie experience such as this. And there's a reason for this: the live-action scenes are made by filmmaker Andrés Borghi, who's worked on a number of films including acclaimed Argentinian horror When Evil Lurks. Borghi has also made games of his own, so there's a natural overlap and understanding there.

It's these live-action sequences that stand Tenebris Somnia apart, that bring another, unusual dimension to it. That pixelated creature rushing toward you in the hallway: it has more substance in your mind now you've seen it up close. That enigmatic lady in a cocktail dress: you know the contours of her face and the strange demonic symbols branded there. You know the world better, more intimately now, and that gives it more impact. These live-action moments also create a kind of propulsive anticipation, knowing that when you do solve a puzzle or unlock that mysterious door, lavish reward and revelation awaits you there.

But I have my doubts about the game overall. Granted, I'm only early on, so perhaps I'm yet to really settle in. But I find its plodding stop-start rhythm works against the suspense and horror. Laboriousness deflates the tension. Frequently revisiting areas robs them of their surprises. And I haven't found much inherent interest in exploring new areas of the game; the writing feels functional to me rather than intriguing, and that's a problem, because in the adventure game sequences it has to do so much of the heavy lifting.

But I remain hopeful. I've watched a little more of Tenebris Somnia than I've managed to play, and the movie-making story at its heart - one focusing on a horror short of your ex's creation - appears to deepen and circle back on itself in interesting ways. Everything you do somehow seems tied to that movie - around deals with devilish entities and the grotesque consequences they bring. That filmic motif repeats again and again. And who knows? Perhaps the live-action sequences represent more than the things our character is experiencing. So there's intrigue there, depth, thought, layers, and, by the looks of it, plenty of imagination to support it.

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S
